Can AI Beat the Pentagon’s Bureaucracy? | Garrett Berntsen, Chief AI Officer, Accenture Federal50 min21 insights2026-09-22
▶ Watch on YouTube
Accenture Federal's Chief AI Officer Garrett Berntsen argues the U-2/Cuban Missile Crisis playbook (fast acquisition plus new institutions to turn raw data into decisions) is the right model for military AI adoption, and that the real 2026 bottlenecks are stale business-system data, hourly-billing procurement incentives, and edge-compute policy risk-aversion, not model capability or token cost.
The U-2 Playbook: Institutions Matter More Than the Sensor · 2Business Systems Are the AI Adoption Bottleneck · 2Procurement, Incentives, and the Economics of Government AI · 7Getting AI to the Edge: Policy Risk as the Real Constraint · 2Where AI Actually Helps Diplomacy and Intelligence · 5AI-Accelerated Cybersecurity Arms Race · 3
The U-2 Playbook: Institutions Matter More Than the Sensor 2
The U-2 spy plane's Cuban Missile Crisis payoff came not from the sensor technology alone but from acquisition reform, fast government-industry collaboration, and new institutions (the NPIC, which later became NGA) built specifically to turn raw imagery into decisions for the president — the same non-technology pieces, not just model access, are what government AI adoption is missing today.
framework🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: highU-2 spy planeCuban Missile CrisisNPICNGAAccenture Federal Services
So what: Federal AI investment theses should weight vendors/programs that fund institutional and workflow reform alongside model access; API licensing alone predicts little actual mission change.
“So that's why APIs alone are not enough.” ▶ 4:24
The U-2 aircraft, designed in the 1950s, reportedly remains in active US service today, with the Pentagon periodically considering decommissioning it and repeatedly deciding not to.
data_point🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: mediumU-2 spy planeDepartment of War
So what: Illustrates how durable a well-designed defense capital asset can be, relevant context for evaluating legacy-platform decommissioning and sustainment-spend debates.
“still in service, actually.” ▶ 2:38
Business Systems Are the AI Adoption Bottleneck 2
Within the Department of War, the most visible AI progress is in operational/battlefield systems like CJADC2 (combined joint all-domain command and control), which gives real-time situational awareness and automated object/intel identification, while 'business systems' — logistics, personnel, finance — lag far behind despite being equally mission-critical.
thesis🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: highCJADC2Department of Warlogistics
So what: Logistics/personnel/finance modernization is a comparatively underexploited federal AI opportunity relative to higher-visibility battlefield-AI deals.
Many DoD logistics reporting systems only require status updates weekly by written policy (Berntsen cites the Army's ~10,000-20,000 supply sergeants as an example), illustrating how much organizational and policy change — not just new software — is needed to get the daily-cadence, AI-ready data that operational-tempo decision systems require.
anecdote🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: highUS Armylogisticssupply sergeants
So what: Vendors selling 'real-time' federal analytics need to bundle policy/workflow redesign into contracts, not just software, since data latency is a structural (not technical) blocker.
“some of these business systems literally the rules say you only have to update the status once a week on some form.” ▶ 10:30
Procurement, Incentives, and the Economics of Government AI 7
Despite widespread talk of AI transforming federal contracting, public federal spending data shows no major shift yet: the government still pays contractors predominantly on a time-and-materials (hourly) basis rather than outcome- or fixed-price, meaning the underlying incentive to bill hours rather than deliver AI-driven efficiency has largely not changed.
data_point🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: highfederal procurementtime-and-materials contracting
So what: Watch federal contract-vehicle spending data for a genuine shift to outcome-based pricing as the real signal that AI is changing government services spend, rather than trusting adoption rhetoric.
“if you actually go into the data on spending it's not a massive shift uh and that data is public you can look at the data uh still most spending is time and materials” ▶ 39:38
Top commercial companies reportedly spend on the order of 10% of their budgets on AI tokens, while the US federal government spends nowhere near that share, suggesting government AI adoption is currently bottlenecked by organizational and contracting reform rather than by compute cost.
data_point🗣 hostconviction: mediumfederal governmentAI tokens
So what: Investors betting that 'cheaper compute unlocks government AI spend' should recalibrate toward organizational-reform and services plays instead of pure compute-cost plays.
“our top companies spending 10% of their budget on tokens And like I don't know where the US government is, but I don't think it's anywhere close to that.” ▶ 39:56
Real AI-driven productivity gains (versus just paying more for the same output via unmanaged token burn) show up mainly in mature teams with disciplined incentive design; immature teams that are incentivized on token usage rather than outcomes risk runaway, low-value token spend.
framework🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: highsoftware developmenttoken economics
So what: When evaluating AI-services vendors, investors should probe team maturity and incentive design, not just tool adoption, to separate real ROI from token-spend theater.
“really mature teams uh I am seeing like large uplift in productivity.” ▶ 40:53
The cost of intelligence (AI capability per dollar) is expected to keep falling because multiple 'Moore's-law-like' curves — infrastructure buildout, model architecture gains, and competitive pricing pressure — are compounding simultaneously, so token spend should not become a binding constraint on government AI adoption.
prediction🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: mediumAI infrastructureMoore's Law
So what: Supports a thesis that compute-cost deflation, not compute-cost scarcity, will be the durable trend shaping federal AI budgets over the next several years.
“we have like 10 other Moors laws going all at the same time for making this intelligence cheaper.” ▶ 41:59
An anecdote about a general arguing that only a drastic 50% budget cut — not a modest trim — forces real bureaucratic change highlights a structural conflict of interest: incumbent vendors like Accenture are never going to recommend cutting the programs they profit from, so meaningful digital-transformation forcing functions may need to come from outside the contractor ecosystem.
contrarian🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: mediumPentagon budgetingJen Pollica
So what: Signals that real DoD transformation may require externally-imposed budget shocks (Congress/OMB) rather than vendor-led modernization, given incumbents' incentive misalignment.
“no 50% we need to cut 50% because that's the only way to get people to change things” ▶ 16:55
Accenture Federal Services is only about 8% of Accenture's overall business, with 92% commercial, which the company uses to import AI deployment lessons (on tokenomics, delivery-team practices, and outcome tracking) learned in commercial engagements into federal work as a 'second mover' advantage before repeating commercial's mistakes.
data_point🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: mediumAccenture Federal ServicesAccenture
So what: Positions large diversified integrators with heavy commercial AI exposure as better positioned to win federal AI contracts than pure-play federal contractors lacking that commercial learning curve.
“Most of our company is actually commercial. 92% of the company is commercial. Only 8% is federal.” ▶ 42:17
Accenture now demonstrates a roughly one-year-old capability — an AI agent configured as a requirements-gathering interviewer that lets a government stakeholder describe a problem conversationally and then builds a working prototype live, shown at Accenture's 'Forge' demo space — though a human engineer still reviews and tweaks the output afterward.
anecdote🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: mediumAccenture Federal ServicesThe Forge
So what: Live AI-driven requirements/prototyping demos are becoming a real sales and adoption lever for federal AI vendors, but full human-out-of-the-loop scoping is not yet here.
“That's only like 12 months old that that's possible.” ▶ 45:15
Getting AI to the Edge: Policy Risk as the Real Constraint 2
Berntsen advocates pushing AI compute (including soldier-carried GPU form factors) directly to service members at the tactical edge, and argues policy must be rewritten so that breaking expensive hardware (he cites roughly $500,000 chips) in the field is treated as an accepted cost of capability delivery rather than a career-ending liability event for the signing officer.
thesis🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: highedge computeGPUsUS military
So what: A coming DoD posture shift toward accepted-risk edge deployment could expand demand for ruggedized/edge AI hardware vendors; the gating factor is policy/liability reform, not technology readiness.
≈ “You have to clear the policy out and just say we're assuming the risk and if it means some GPUs get broken and we lose the money. Well, it was worth it cuz we're getting capability out to the edge.” paraphrase
US enlisted service members and civil servants are, on average, more educated than the general American population and roughly representative of the country's demographic composition, which Berntsen argues undercuts the assumption that a fully mobilized population (as in Ukraine) is required for bottom-up battlefield innovation — Ukraine's innovation edge instead comes from survival necessity, not a broader talent pool.
contrarian🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: mediumUS militaryUkraine
So what: Argues the current all-volunteer US force already has sufficient underlying talent for emergent, soldier-driven AI adoption; the binding constraint is incentive structures and policy, not personnel quality.
“they're actually on average more educated than the average American” ▶ 25:06
Where AI Actually Helps Diplomacy and Intelligence 5
Berntsen predicts that within roughly five years, many business negotiations will become largely automated as 'two models arriving at a number that works for both parties,' extending existing algorithmic pricing patterns (ride-hailing meters, Facebook Marketplace-style offers) from micro-transactions to broader commercial deals.
prediction🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: mediumnegotiation automationUberFacebook Marketplace
So what: Flags a plausible near-term market for AI-mediated commercial negotiation/pricing tools, distinct from higher-stakes political negotiation.
“I would not be shocked if 5 years from now business negotiations are just two models arriving at a number that works for both parties.” ▶ 31:45
Large country-to-country and high-stakes political/economic negotiations will be the last category automated by AI, because effective diplomacy relies on relationship-building and personal engagement (Berntsen cites historical negotiator Richard Holbrooke's late-night calls and in-person cajoling) that resists being reduced to a model, and foreign counterparts will resist negotiating with an AI-generated output.
contrarian🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: mediumRichard Holbrookediplomacy
So what: For anyone building 'AI replaces negotiation' products, the realistic near-term market is retail/commercial transactions, not geopolitics — sequencing matters for go-to-market strategy.
“people like doing business with people.” ▶ 0:47
Berntsen is skeptical that AI can be meaningfully applied to model strategic, situation-room-level decision-making (e.g., simulating what offer to put on the table to a counterparty like Iran) because the necessary data — private notes, internal emails, and negotiator audio/video from every situation room — is deliberately withheld and effectively unobtainable, unlike intelligence analysis.
framework🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: mediumintelligence communitynegotiation modelingIran
So what: Distinguishes a near-term addressable federal AI application (intel-analysis quality scoring) from a much harder, longer-horizon one (strategic decision simulation) for prioritizing where AI-evaluation contracts will actually land.
“if you could have absolute enormous access to people's private notes and emails and all the audio and the video from every situation room, you know, obviously this would never happen.” ▶ 27:58
Intelligence analysis is a more tractable near-term AI application than strategic decision modeling because the intel community already has structured SOPs, standardized training on report-writing, and decades of graded outcome data that could be used to benchmark what constitutes high- versus low-quality analytic tradecraft.
framework🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: highintelligence community
So what: Points to intel-analysis quality-scoring/automation as a concrete, data-supported near-term federal AI use case worth tracking for contract awards and vendor wins.
“if you have decades of data on that and you have some signals of what was good and what was bad and what's high quality and what's low quality, then you can start to net out” ▶ 29:41
Jordan Schneider speculates that AI could eventually reshape electoral politics if a candidate wins by fully outsourcing campaign strategy to a model, at which point the contest shifts to 'which model' — a heavily guardrailed model that avoids negative attacks versus an unguardrailed model willing to recommend attack ads or fabricated claims — turning AI alignment choices into a direct political weapon.
prediction🗣 hostconviction: exploratorypolitical campaignsAI guardrails
So what: Flags a future regulatory and reputational risk vector for AI model providers if guardrail design choices become directly implicated in electoral outcomes.
AI-Accelerated Cybersecurity Arms Race 3
Berntsen frames the current AI-driven cybersecurity moment — discussed two days after an OpenAI model's security guardrails were reportedly broken, around July 2026 — as accelerating pre-existing, already-known vulnerabilities rather than creating fundamentally new risk categories: AI tools now let attackers find known vulnerability classes far faster, compressing defenders' response window.
causal🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: highOpenAIcybersecurity
So what: Raises urgency for enterprises and government to compress patch cycles; watch for rising demand in AI-driven vulnerability triage and automated hardening tools, on both the offense and defense side.
“what we're doing is we're putting lighter fluid on things we've already been talking about.” ▶ 0:00
Berntsen states there is not a huge capability lag between Chinese open-source models (referencing a model he calls 'Kimi') and top-tier US frontier models, which adds pressure on US government and enterprise cyber-defense timelines since adversarial AI capability cannot be assumed to trail meaningfully.
data_point🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: mediumKimiChinese open-source modelsfrontier US models
So what: Undercuts any federal cyber-AI budgeting assumption of a large, durable US lead over Chinese open models, per a practitioner working active federal AI/cyber engagements.
“there's not a huge lag between the pace of the Chinese open source models and our uh mythos grade models” ▶ 37:24
Organizations must shift from monthly to near-daily patch cadences and use AI itself to triage the large backlogs of known vulnerabilities every CISO/CIO already has and to write hardening code, because human-scale manual triage cannot keep pace with AI-accelerated vulnerability discovery.
framework🗣 Garrett Berntsenconviction: highCISOCIOcybersecurity
So what: Practical operating-model shift (faster patch cycles, AI-assisted triage) that federal and enterprise security budgets will need to fund; a leading indicator for demand in AI-native vulnerability-management vendors.
“you just have to be able to tell your user base, sorry, like your computer's going to reboot every day.” ▶ 37:50

So what: Record fixed-income inflows at a 5% yield signal a structural shift back toward bonds after years of TINA-driven equity flows; watch whether this flow persists if yields push higher.
“There is a record pace of money flows into bonds through August. uh 600 plus billion dollars into fixed income” ▶ 22:56
Both hosts personally bought bonds for the first time in years (Ben two weeks ago, Michael one week ago, and for Michael effectively the first time ever outside a 529 target-date fund), citing the roughly 5% 10-year yield as an attractive, prudent entry point not seen since before the Great Financial Crisis.
anecdote🗣 hostsconviction: high
So what: Two professional advisers independently re-entering bonds at 5% is a real-money signal that the risk/reward on duration has flipped positive after years of near-zero yields.
“We haven't seen 5% yields since before the great financial crisis.” ▶ 24:54
A Goldman Sachs chart shows equities have enjoyed a near-record period of outperformance relative to bonds (both US and world portfolios), the widest spread since the 1950s-1960s, meaning most investors have drifted to be underweight bonds simply through natural portfolio drift.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumGoldman Sachs
So what: Portfolios that haven't been actively rebalanced are likely far more equity-heavy than intended after this stretch; a rebalance back toward bonds at current yields is a straightforward, actionable move.
“equities have enjoyed a near record period of outperformance relative to bonds” ▶ 25:09
With 10-year yields near 5%, a 60/40 portfolio now only needs about 8% annual returns from its stock allocation to hit a 7% blended target, versus needing roughly 11% from stocks when bonds yielded 1-2% in the early 2020s.
framework🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: medium
So what: Investors who overweighted equities (e.g., shifted from 60/40 to 70/30, 80/20, or 90/10) during the zero-rate years to hit return targets can now consider dialing risk back down toward 60/40 given higher bond yields do more of the work.
“the people who did take more risk now can hopefully think, oh, I went from I should have been 6040, I went 7030 or 8020 or 9010. Now I can maybe get back to 7030 or 6040 because these things are more in alignment.” ▶ 27:10
Goldman Sachs (via Mike Zakardi) modeled 10-year S&P 500 return scenarios ranging from stagflation and stagnation to a 'Goldilocks' base case, with a best case of Goldilocks-plus-uninterrupted-AI-boom producing roughly 14% annualized S&P returns over the next decade.
prediction🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: lowGoldman SachsMike Zakardi
So what: 14%/year for a decade would be the best 10-year run in market history; both hosts assign it only roughly a 10-11% probability, useful as a sanity check against overly bullish long-run return assumptions in financial plans.
“if we get a Goldilocks plus an AI boom that doesn't falter, we could be seeing 14% annual returns in the S&P for the next 10 years” ▶ 27:59
Why stock-picking keeps getting harder 4
Adam Parker (Trivariate Research), in a piece titled 'Buy and Hold Doesn't Work,' found that only 23% of the top 500 US stocks held for a full 10-year period beat the S&P 500 index, in the latest observation.
data_point🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: highAdam ParkerTrivariate Research
So what: A 23% hit rate for individual stocks beating the index over 10 years is a strong quantitative argument for indexing over long-horizon single-stock buy-and-hold strategies.
“Adam says only 23% of the top 500 US stocks held for 10 years beat the index in the latest observation.” ▶ 31:18
Over a shorter 3-year holding period, the hit rate for an individual stock beating the S&P 500 rises to 27.7%, and the best odds of all are over a 1-year horizon, since short-term dislocations let oversold stocks bounce even if they're structurally poor long-term holdings.
data_point🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: high
So what: If attempting individual stock selection at all, shorter holding periods have statistically better odds of outperformance than multi-year buy-and-hold, though this cuts against classic long-term investing advice.
“for three year for threeear holdings the hit rate is 27.7%.” ▶ 31:28
The same low hit-rate dynamic for stocks beating the index over 10 years holds even within the broader top-2,000-stock universe, not just among S&P 500 mega-caps, meaning it isn't merely an artifact of extreme concentration in a handful of winners like Nvidia and Apple.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumNvidiaApple
So what: Rules out the objection that concentration in a few mega-cap winners explains the poor stock-picking odds — the difficulty of beating the index is structural and broad-based, reinforcing the case for indexing.
“It's the same exact thing in the top 2,000 stocks.” ▶ 31:45
Ben Carlson argues buy-and-hold single-stock investing used to be more viable decades ago (e.g., dividend-focused investors in the 1930s-1950s) but has become effectively impossible now because industries and company fortunes change too fast for a 10-year hold to reliably work.
thesis🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: medium
So what: Reinforces a structural (not cyclical) shift toward indexing/active turnover over long-horizon concentrated stock bets as the pace of business-model disruption accelerates.
“it probably used to be easier to be an individual stock buy and hold investor. And a lot of people in the past did it in like the 30s, 40s, and 50s.” ▶ 31:02

The Wall Street Journal reported aggregate consumer prices are up 27% since January 2021, but many everyday categories are up 40-60% over the same period, and roughly a third of the items in the CPI basket are still rising more than 4% year-over-year even though the weighted average is about 3.5%.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: highWall Street Journal
So what: Headline CPI understates the lived inflation experience for many households because a meaningful share of the basket is running well above target — helps explain persistent consumer inflation frustration despite moderating headline prints.
“a third of the consumer basket is still rising at more than 4% year-over-year.” ▶ 37:22
There is no literal 'K-shape' in the wage/spending data historically, but the pattern that upper-income households have structurally outperformed lower-income households over time is real and longstanding, not a new post-pandemic phenomenon.
framework🗣 hostsconviction: medium
So what: Useful corrective for interpreting 'K-shaped economy' headlines — the underlying inequality trend is real but the specific 'K' visual/narrative is overstated media framing.
“there was never anything in the shape of a K like they people say K-shaped economy because it's a good narrative but no chart ever actually looked like a K.” ▶ 37:57
As of August, after-tax wage growth for lower-income households was outpacing that of middle- and higher-income households (a dynamic that has flipped multiple times this cycle), but because lower-income households spend a disproportionate share of their budget on energy, much of that wage gain is being offset by higher gas prices.
causal🗣 Ben Carlson, citing Mike Zakardiconviction: mediumMike Zakardi
So what: Nominal wage-growth leadership by lower-income cohorts is real but largely illusory in purchasing-power terms right now due to energy-price exposure — worth watching energy prices as a direct drag on lower-income real incomes.
“there's a way bigger consumption of energy prices in a budget for lower income households.” ▶ 38:34
Initial unemployment claims are down 10% year-over-year and continuing claims are falling, indicating labor-market strength that runs counter to fears that AI is already displacing large numbers of workers.

If GDP growth stays modest while the stock market keeps rising, the Buffett Indicator (market cap to GDP) will become even more stretched relative to history, but the hosts argue there's no inherent reason the ratio must revert to past norms since more of the economy's capital now flows through and is captured in public equity markets over time.

Private markets, illiquidity, and fraud risk 4
NFL/NBA athletes Travis Kelce, Tim Hardaway Jr., Gary Harris, and Mason Plumlee were investors in a Ponzi scheme run through an entity called Swift Arc LLC, per Detroit News reporting; the operator raised tens of millions claiming to make venture-capital-style investments but actually put money into Philip Morris Pakistan stock and personal spending, paying earlier investors with new investor funds.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: highTravis KelceTim Hardaway Jr.Gary HarrisMason PlumleeSwift Arc LLC
So what: A concrete, named case study of a private-investment Ponzi scheme reaching high-profile, presumably sophisticated investors — a reminder that celebrity/athlete investor involvement is not a signal of legitimacy.
“There was this Swift Arc LLC. The guy took in tens of millions of dollars.” ▶ 49:02
Private and illiquid investments are structurally easier vehicles for fraud than public markets because they offer a 'black box' lack of transparency and investors cannot force liquidity to test whether the underlying assets/returns are real.
framework🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: high
So what: As private-market allocations grow in both institutional and retail (e.g., evergreen fund) portfolios, investors should weight illiquidity/opacity itself as a distinct fraud-risk factor, not just a return/liquidity tradeoff.
“it's so much easier to commit fraud in private investments. You can have the whole aura of blackbox in anything illquid.” ▶ 49:31
A Wall Street Journal story on Mark Walter (the investor who sold the Lakers) described a surge of money into annuities, including a case where an investor who bought annuities from a company for decades later discovered the issuer had been investing the proceeds in unauthorized ventures — despite insurance/annuities being one of the most heavily regulated industries.
anecdote🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumMark WalterWall Street Journal
So what: Shows that even in highly regulated products like annuities, fraud can persist undetected for decades — regulation alone is not sufficient protection, reinforcing the case for direct due diligence on where annuity/insurance premiums are actually invested.
“you'd think that insurance would be one of the most regulated industries that there is. And I think it is. And it was still easy for this guy to do this.” ▶ 50:10
Most private-market fraud likely goes unreported because victims are reluctant to admit publicly that they were taken advantage of, so cases only surface when litigation forces disclosure — meaning known Ponzi/fraud cases likely represent a fraction of actual occurrences.
causal🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: medium
So what: Investors should assume reported private-market fraud cases are a lower bound, not the full picture, when sizing the risk of allocating to opaque, illiquid strategies.
“a lot of fraud you never hear about because unless it goes to court because people don't want to admit that they got taken advantage of.” ▶ 50:33

data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumLakers

Going from a $12 billion purchase price to a $30 billion target valuation over 10 years works out to roughly a 10% annualized return — lower than the hosts' initial gut estimate of 12-14%, since a simple double in 10 years equates to about 7.2% annualized (the 'Rule of 72').
framework🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: high
So what: A useful quick mental-math check (Rule of 72: years to double is roughly 72/rate) for sanity-testing any 'X billion to Y billion in Z years' valuation claim in deal coverage before taking the headline multiple at face value.
“12 to 30 is a 10% annual return.” ▶ 58:06
Apollo is buying a 16% stake in the New York Yankees with both a debt (private credit) and an equity component reportedly valued at well over $12 billion (per Charles Gasparino, New York Post); the hosts question why a purely passive equity investor (as opposed to a prestige buyer like Iger/Kushner with the Lakers) would accept these implied returns versus simply owning liquid public equities.
contrarian🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumApolloYankeesGasparino

data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumMLBApollo
So what: An expected relaxation of PE ownership caps in MLB is a structural catalyst that could accelerate institutional capital inflows and further re-rate franchise valuations across the league, not just the Yankees.
“Pe firms are currently barred from owning more than 15% of any team according to MLB rules, though the league is expected to wave the cap.” ▶ 1:00:43
Bitcoin was down 33% year-to-date as of late July but has rallied back to roughly flat for the year around $86,000, a move the hosts attribute not to the Clarity Act (which actually failed to pass) but to a self-reinforcing price/sentiment recovery correlated with a broader software/tech stock bounce.
data_point🗣 hostsconviction: mediumBitcoin
So what: Bitcoin's rebound appears driven by pure price momentum/sentiment rather than the regulatory catalyst (Clarity Act) commonly cited, suggesting its near-term direction may track risk-asset/software sentiment more than policy news.
“No, no, no, not because of regulation. The Clarity Act hopes for regulation. The Clarity Act failed.” ▶ 44:40
Ben Carlson's long-term bull case for Bitcoin is behavioral: every time sentiment gets maximally bearish and the asset 'feels dead,' it stages a sharp comeback, which he frames as evidence buyers can re-enter quickly without needing a specific fundamental catalyst.
thesis🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumBitcoin
So what: Frames Bitcoin's resilience as a sentiment/positioning phenomenon rather than a fundamentals-driven one — useful for traders who want to fade extreme bearish sentiment readings in the asset.
“this is the the best long-term bullcase that every time it feels like it's just dead than dead, it comes back.” ▶ 45:37
Housing supply, construction inflation, and labor competition 2
Home-builder executives quoted by the new 'Housing Frame' Substack (Connor Sand) say labor availability has become a growing constraint, citing immigration enforcement and 'enthusiastic data center construction' both competing for the same construction workforce, pushing up build costs even as builders offset some of it with efficiencies of scale.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumConnor SandHousing Frame
So what: Data-center buildout and immigration enforcement are directly competing with homebuilding for scarce construction labor, a supply-side cost pressure investors in homebuilders and building-products companies should track alongside interest rates.
“Lair labor availability has started to become more of an issue. Immigration enforcement, enthusiastic data center construction continue to create tightness in certain geographies. We've been able to offset labor increases with efficiencies of scale, but the pressure on cost is certainly building.” ▶ 46:17
If policymakers removed regulatory barriers and fully incentivized building enough homes to close the estimated 3-4 million unit housing shortage, the surge in construction demand for labor and materials would itself likely cause a significant new bout of cost inflation, meaning the simple 'just build more housing' fix has an underappreciated inflationary side effect.
causal🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: medium
So what: Complicates the popular 'build more supply solves the housing crisis' narrative — any serious housing-supply push should be expected to come with a construction-cost/inflation offset that policymakers and investors should price in.
“If we really were to to fill in the hole with those 3 to four million houses were missing, if we could somehow do it, it would just cause another crisis.” ▶ 47:21

Capital Allocators · Ted Seides 1 episode(s) · 45 insights

André Perold: Why Alignment Drift – Not Performance – Is the Real Exit Trigger62 min45 insights2026-09-23
▶ Watch on YouTube
Andre Perold of High Vista Strategies argues that in an era of simultaneous exponential change (AI, GLP-1 drugs, US fiscal deficits), edge comes from partnering with narrow specialists in 'beautifully inefficient' small-scale markets, and that the real trigger to exit a successful manager is alignment/capacity drift as assets grow, not performance.
Investing Through Exponential Change · 8Portfolio Architecture: From One Fund to a Multi-Strategy Platform · 5The Real Exit Trigger: Alignment and Capacity Drift, Not Performance · 6Private Equity's DPI Problem and the Continuation-Vehicle Opportunity · 7AI as an Internal Deal-Screening Tool · 4Private Credit: Getting Paid for Complexity, Not Risk · 8Venture Capital as an AI Proxy · 3Biotech: Capacity-Constrained Small-Cap Specialists · 2Overlooked Regional and Niche Opportunities · 2
Investing Through Exponential Change 8
Andre Perold sees the current environment as the most unique and exciting investing environment of his career because multiple forces (AI, GLP-1 healthcare drugs, US fiscal deficits) are exponential simultaneously, unlike single-shock events like COVID.
thesis🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh VistaAIGLP-1 drugsUS budget deficit
So what: Frame current markets as a multi-vector exponential regime rather than a single-theme bubble when sizing risk and time horizon.
“I think it's the most exciting investment environment that I've ever seen. I think it's the most uncertain environment in certain ways that I've ever seen.” ▶ 0:36
Perold argues that rapid, exponential change makes it easier (not harder) to find investment edge, because slow-moving, well-followed businesses like old-line General Motors get efficiently priced while fast-changing situations are harder for the market to digest.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highGeneral MotorsAI
So what: Favor sectors undergoing rapid structural change over stable, well-covered incumbents when hunting for mispricings.
Perold deliberately separates his view of the world (a large US deficit and rapid disruption are societal problems for the next generation) from his investing lens (the same dynamics are highly profitable), arguing investors must consciously wear two different hats.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumUS budget deficit
So what: Don't let moral or macro pessimism about deficits/disruption bleed into portfolio positioning; the two lenses can diverge.
“You got to separate. You got to wear two hats.” ▶ 3:39
The US federal debt grew from about $5 trillion in the early 2000s to about $40 trillion today, and the current deficit run-rate is around $2 trillion and growing, which Perold cites as evidence of exponential fiscal dynamics.
data_point🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highUS budget deficit
So what: Use as a concrete anchor for how fast US debt has compounded when modeling long-run rate, inflation, or fiscal-crisis scenarios.
“The US budget, the US debt was 5 trillion in 200 early 2000s. It's 40 trillion. It's exponential.” ▶ 3:11
Perold contends high valuations in AI-related names are not automatically a red flag; a low price given a supposedly transformative technology would actually be the more suspicious signal, though he explicitly flags 'this time is different' as historically the most dangerous phrase in investing.
contrarian🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumAI
So what: Weigh whether a market's pricing is internally consistent with its own growth narrative rather than dismissing high multiples outright as overvaluation.
“those are the most dangerous words in investing is saying this time is different but this time is different” ▶ 5:34
High Vista structures a dedicated 'AI sleeve' of the portfolio built on the assumption AI will be transformational, paired with an explicit 'anti-AI' sleeve of non-AI-exposed strategies, to avoid the near-impossible task of comparing AI and non-AI opportunities against each other day to day.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh VistaAI
So what: A structural template LPs/allocators can copy: bucket a portfolio into an explicit AI-thesis sleeve and a deliberately non-AI sleeve rather than trying to rank all opportunities on one scale.
“I'm going to create an AI sleeve on the assumption it will be very different” ▶ 5:44
Perold argues humans systematically underestimate exponential phenomena, citing that when High Vista staff were asked early in COVID to forecast case counts, every guess was far too low, and applies the same blind spot to today's AI disruption forecasts.
causal🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumAI
So what: Treat consensus AI-adoption and disruption timelines as likely too conservative, given a documented human tendency to underestimate exponential curves.
“We're very bad at exponential math” ▶ 7:03
High Vista's core answer to needing an edge amid exponential change is partnering with increasingly narrow specialists (e.g., hard-tech experts on AI compute, roofing-industry operators for a small buyout) rather than trying to cover broad themes generically.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh VistaAIcompute
So what: In fast-moving themes, edge comes from teaming with domain-narrow specialists (e.g., compute/hardware experts) rather than generalist thematic exposure.
Portfolio Architecture: From One Fund to a Multi-Strategy Platform 5
High Vista started in 2004 as a single multi-asset, top-down/bottom-up global opportunity fund, and later spun out dedicated strategies (biotech, small buyouts, venture, private credit, continuation vehicles) as plain-vanilla approaches like simple value investing got competed away by increasingly sophisticated LPs and managers.
causal🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vista
So what: Rising sophistication among asset owners (sovereign wealth funds, family offices, endowments) is compressing returns to simple public-market strategies, pushing allocators toward niche, specialist structures.
“Your value investing was a great strategy. Simple value investing was a great strategy. Today that's no longer interesting.” ▶ 11:30
Perold defines his target opportunity set as 'beautifully inefficient markets': large numbers of small, deeply specialized opportunities protected by a moat of limited capacity (too small for big capital to compete for), distinct from 'ugly' inefficient markets like parts of Africa where expropriation and insider transfer pricing destroy returns even when the underlying business thesis is right.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vista
So what: A reusable screen for allocators: seek inefficiency caused by scale/capacity limits (a durable moat), not inefficiency caused by institutional/political risk (an unrewarded risk).
“The beautifully inefficient ones are the ones where it's very clean where if you can figure it out, it'll work, but it has to needs a moat from competition” ▶ 50:51
High Vista runs two distinct portfolio processes: a daily bottom-up alpha-sourcing process across specialist strategies, followed by a separate beta-management process that aggregates the market exposures embedded in each alpha position and trues the portfolio up toward desired (largely US-tilted, broad market-cap-weighted) beta exposure.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vista
So what: A concrete two-stage portfolio construction model (source alpha first, manage aggregate beta second) that separates security/manager selection from top-down risk budgeting.
“so think of their two investment processes the main one what we do every day come to work bottom up find alpha” ▶ 48:44
High Vista grew its strategy lineup through both organic build (biotech and private credit) and acquisition (the small-buyout/PE team and the venture/continuation-vehicle capability), with the decision driven by sourcing capability, underwriting capability, available talent, and whether a durable moat exists.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumHigh Vista
So what: A build-vs-buy checklist for asset managers expanding into new niches: sourcing edge, underwriting edge, people availability, and defensibility.
As High Vista's sourcing has become more geographically dispersed and remote, it deliberately holds four to six mandatory in-person gatherings a year (town halls, deep-dive investment sessions, company outings, strategy sessions on topics like data and AI) to preserve a culture of rigorous, frank debate.
anecdote🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumHigh Vista
So what: A practical culture-maintenance cadence for distributed, sourcing-driven investment teams competing for talent against fully remote or fully in-office peers.
The Real Exit Trigger: Alignment and Capacity Drift, Not Performance 6
High Vista's decision to exit a manager relationship is almost never driven by poor performance; it is driven by a breakdown in alignment of interests, most commonly when a manager's assets under management grow to the point where the original strategy's edge (built on small size/limited capacity) is compromised.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vista
So what: When reviewing existing manager relationships, weight AUM growth and capacity drift more heavily than trailing performance as the primary red flag for redemption.
“It's very seldom performance. It's more that the alignment isn't there.” ▶ 16:04
High Vista's due-diligence test for a manager whose assets have grown is to ask them to demonstrate they are no longer capacity-constrained by their larger denominator; a credible answer (e.g., proof the strategy was always more liquid than assumed) can satisfy the concern, while evidence of genuine capacity degradation cannot.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vista
So what: A concrete diligence question LPs can put directly to growing managers: prove the strategy's edge scales with assets, don't just assert it.
“Show me that at this larger denominator you can still do really well. That show me you're no longer constrained by the denominator.” ▶ 16:17
In venture capital, choosing not to re-up in a manager's next fund is effectively a terminal decision to end the relationship (unlike a hedge fund where an LP can partially redeem), which raises the stakes and required deliberation around every venture manager review.
causal🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highventure capitalHigh Vista
So what: Venture LP manager reviews need a higher evidentiary bar before non-renewal, since the relationship cannot be trimmed incrementally like a liquid hedge fund position.
“It's not continuing is basically saying we're done. This is how it works. It's not like in a hedge fund you can take out half your position.” ▶ 33:58
A specific red flag Perold looks for in venture managers is a firm raising substantially larger funds without adding partners or resources, which changes what the firm is actually doing in a way that should trigger scrutiny of whether the same edge still applies.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumventure capital
So what: Screen venture manager updates for fund-size growth unmatched by team growth as an early alignment-drift signal, before performance deteriorates.
“they haven't added any partners but they're raising a ton of money, but the organization isn't adapting to the capital flowing in.” ▶ 35:02
High Vista's most common investment pattern across biotech, small buyouts and venture is backing talented investors early in their independent careers right after they spin out from larger firms, when motivation is highest, rather than chasing already-established brand-name managers.
thesis🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vistabiotechprivate equityventure capital
So what: Track spin-out talent from large biotech, PE and VC franchises as a repeatable sourcing pipeline rather than waiting for managers to establish long track records.
“Most investments we've ever made are working with partners that are early in their careers in that entity.” ▶ 19:04
Perold frames unusually easy access to a sought-after venture manager as itself a warning sign, invoking the 'Groucho Marx problem' that a club willing to have you may not be the club worth joining.
contrarian🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumventure capital
So what: Treat easy access to a hot-sounding manager as a diligence flag, not a lucky break, when allocating to competitive strategies.
“It's the Groucho Marks problem. Why do you want to join a club that'll have you? But that's called investing.” ▶ 37:18
Private Equity's DPI Problem and the Continuation-Vehicle Opportunity 7
PE is facing a structural 'DPI problem': portfolio companies are staying privately held for longer (often passed between successive private owners rather than sold or IPO'd), realizations are scarce, and GPs that overpaid a few years ago are struggling to clear return hurdles and generate carry.
thesis🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highprivate equity
So what: Expect continued pressure on PE carry realization and fundraising for vintages that bought at peak multiples; watch DPI trends as a leading indicator of GP franchise health.
“It's called the DPI problem.” ▶ 19:43
Perold argues that companies which reach public markets overwhelmingly originate from venture capital, not classic buyout private equity, because PE's core function is concentrated private ownership to reposition a business (from state A to state B), not to prepare it for an IPO.
thesis🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumprivate equityventure capital
So what: Reframes the PE asset class as structurally a private-to-private ownership vehicle, implying LPs should underwrite DPI mainly via strategic/sponsor-to-sponsor sales and continuation vehicles, not IPO exits.
“the public firms come out of VC. You look at the best firms in the market, they came out of VC, not out of PE. So PE is not a breeding ground for public firms.” ▶ 20:01
The DPI and carry-generation problem is worst at the large-cap end of private equity, where entry multiples are highest, and is comparatively much less severe in the lower middle market where High Vista concentrates its small-buyout strategy.
data_point🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highprivate equity
So what: Segment PE exposure by deal size when assessing DPI risk; lower middle market managers may be structurally better positioned than large-cap buyout funds right now.
“It's toughest at the very large end where the entry multiples are the highest and the carry problem is the highest. We have found the DPI is much less of an issue in the lower middle market” ▶ 29:22
The shrinking-carry dynamic at larger PE firms is pushing experienced dealmakers to spin out as 'independent' or 'fundless' sponsors, and High Vista estimates roughly 2,000 such fundless or small sponsors currently exist, a universe it tracks almost in its entirety as a sourcing edge.
data_point🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vistaprivate equity
So what: A quantified, trackable universe (~2,000 fundless/small sponsors) that specialist LPs can use to benchmark the depth of their own PE sourcing network.
“There's maybe 2,000 such either fundless or small sponsors in total around. We follow almost all of them and you only appreciate it when you see what we can do when you have that breadth.” ▶ 18:44
Continuation vehicles are emerging as a major opportunity because GPs holding genuinely strong ('gem') businesses want to keep compounding them rather than sell, but face LP pressure to show DPI; High Vista built a dedicated team to directly source and underwrite continuation-vehicle deals, concentrating on the lower middle market where multiple-expansion runway is greatest.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vistaprivate equity
So what: Continuation vehicles in the lower middle market are a structurally growing, self-selecting access point to a GP's best-performing assets rather than a dumping ground for weak ones.
“Why do you want to sell it? Someone has to take that business and continue to morph it. And you in the best position to do that.” ▶ 23:11
Not all continuation-vehicle deal flow involves genuinely strong businesses, but Perold believes weak assets are unlikely to clear the bar needed to actually execute a real continuation vehicle, tilting realized CV deal flow toward better-quality companies even though it isn't universal.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumprivate equity
So what: Apply selection-bias logic when screening CV deal flow: completed CVs skew toward quality, but diligence should still confirm each specific deal rather than assume the label guarantees it.
“it's going to be hard to do a real CV if it's not a gem.” ▶ 24:47
Co-investment underwriting is structurally harder than backing a GP's commingled fund because each co-invest deal is typically novel for the sponsor as well as for High Vista, requiring deep bespoke diligence, whereas fund commitments benefit from an established multi-deal track record with that sponsor.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumHigh Vistaprivate equity
So what: LPs should apply a higher diligence bar to co-investments than to blind-pool fund commitments with the same sponsor, since co-invests lack the sponsor's track-record cushion.
“A co-invest with a sponsor, it's usually an investment they're making that is new for them. So, it's new for them, it's new for us.” ▶ 25:22
AI as an Internal Deal-Screening Tool 4
High Vista built an internal AI agent, developed by co-head of PE Scott Reed, that reads incoming deal emails, decks and attachments, auto-summarizes each opportunity, and scores it against a defined risk scorecard (e.g., client concentration) inside a secured internal AI sandbox before a human analyst gets involved.
data_point🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh VistaScott ReedAI
So what: A concrete production example of AI-driven deal triage in private markets that other allocators/GPs can benchmark against when building their own inbound-deal screening tools.
“It's an AI agent that gets takes the takes the decks, gets industry information, gets information about the sponsor”
High Vista is backtesting its AI deal-scoring robot against historical deals to check whether unaided AI scoring would have correctly identified past winners before trusting it to filter live pipeline without human intervention.
data_point🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: exploratoryHigh VistaAI
So what: A signal that sophisticated allocators are moving from 'try AI on live deals' to formal backtested validation before granting AI tools more autonomy in the investment process.
“we're running a back test to see if we had done this in the past, how well would it have done without any intervention” ▶ 28:22
Perold observes that AI adoption across High Vista's investment teams has been organic and self-motivated rather than mandated from the top, which he treats as a leading indicator of the firm's ability to keep an edge as the competitive landscape changes rapidly.
anecdote🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumHigh VistaAI
So what: Bottom-up, voluntary AI tool-building within an investment team can be used as a qualitative proxy for organizational adaptability when evaluating a manager.
“The team naturally is adopting AI, not because you're pushing them into it, but because they're finding it's just helpful.” ▶ 58:16
Perold personally plans to spend his next few years using AI coding tools like Claude Code to automate his own calendar and email workflows, describing it as returning to his earlier career as a hands-on programmer.
anecdote🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumClaude CodeAI
So what: An anecdotal data point that even senior, non-technical-facing allocators are personally hands-on with AI coding tools, suggesting deeper organizational AI fluency than title alone implies.
“I was a pretty hardcore programmer back in the day and you play with Claude code Claude co-work vibe code it's amazing” ▶ 1:01:35
Private Credit: Getting Paid for Complexity, Not Risk 8
Perold frames all private credit as fundamentally 'a contract' along a spectrum from unsecured direct lending to highly collateralized bespoke structures, and argues High Vista's edge comes specifically from being paid for solving a borrower's complex problem, not from being paid for taking uncollateralized risk.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vistaprivate credit
So what: A useful mental model for evaluating any private credit manager: ask whether the manager's return is compensation for complexity/structuring work or simply compensation for unsecured risk.
“our credit strategy is always based on where can I find great borrowers” ▶ 38:21
High Vista structurally avoids uncollateralized corporate/sponsor direct lending because it is a commoditized, capital-flooded segment with little edge, and in a downside scenario the lender is left renegotiating restructuring terms directly against the PE sponsor that owns the borrower.
contrarian🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vistaprivate credit
So what: Be skeptical of direct-lending strategies marketed as differentiated; per Perold, the segment is commoditized and returns may not compensate for downside negotiating leverage against sponsors.
“We've always avoided direct lending.” ▶ 44:15
Perold believes market concern about private credit's exposure to struggling software borrowers is overblown, because the loans are short-dated and only need the borrower to remain viable for a few years, unlike equity, where a software company's long-run 'terminal value' amid AI disruption is the critical and highly uncertain variable.
contrarian🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highprivate creditAI
So what: Differentiate credit risk from equity risk when assessing AI-disruption exposure in software: short-dated credit may be insulated from long-horizon terminal-value uncertainty that is crushing software equity multiples.
“We think it's overblown. We think a lot of the software will be okay cuz the credits are short-term loans.” ▶ 39:05
Perold cites Salesforce as a case study in terminal-value uncertainty: its market cap peaked above $300 billion around October 2025 and has since fallen about 40% despite continued strong earnings growth guided through roughly 2028, because investors can't price its value beyond that horizon amid AI disruption fears.
data_point🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highSalesforceAI
So what: A live example of AI-disruption fear compressing equity multiples on a company with intact near-term fundamentals, useful for calibrating how much of current software-sector weakness is earnings-driven versus multiple-driven.
“Its market cap peaked over 300 billion. It's down by 40%. The peak was last October.” ▶ 39:48
Ted Seides raises a risk that 'short-dated' private credit safety assumptions depend on there being someone else to refinance or take out the loan; Perold's response is that with sufficient collateral coverage a lender can absorb a fair hit and still recover principal through negotiated extension, rather than relying on an active refinancing/default cycle.
causal🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumprivate credit
So what: When underwriting private credit funds, weight collateral coverage and recovery math more heavily than assumptions about loan tenor or an active refinancing market.
“But if you're sufficiently collateralized, then the issue is you can take a fair hit and still get your money back.” ▶ 41:29
High Vista's most attractive current private credit niche is 'GP solutions' lending — structured loans that help GPs meet fund commitment and capital-call obligations — where the firm's long-standing GP relationships give it a sourcing and underwriting edge over generalist lenders.
thesis🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vistaprivate creditprivate equity
So what: GP-solutions lending is a growing, relationship-gated niche within private credit worth tracking as GP capital needs (driven by the broader DPI problem) increase.
“GPS have a need for money. GPS have to commit to their funds.” ▶ 42:36
Data-center construction lending is a standout current private-credit niche: highly collateralized construction loans with deposit-based risk mechanics, tied indirectly to hyperscaler credit, are attractive because end-investor capital available for data-center financing is limited relative to the buildout need.
data_point🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highprivate creditAI
So what: Data-center construction debt is a relatively underserved corner of the AI infrastructure buildout that specialist private credit managers can access ahead of generalist capital.
“But the data center space is fantastic. It's interesting. The collateral is directly or indirectly related to a hyperscaler often.” ▶ 43:54
Perold notes that energy lending, previously a strong niche for High Vista, has become crowded now that 'everyone's back into energy,' making it explicitly less attractive today than newer niches like data centers and GP solutions.
contrarian🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumprivate credit
So what: Treat crowding language ('everyone's back into X') from a specialist manager as a direct signal that a previously strong niche's risk-adjusted return has likely compressed.
“Energy was fantastic. These days energy is everyone's back into energy. Energy is in vogue. So it's less interesting.” ▶ 43:14
Venture Capital as an AI Proxy 3
Perold argues that most of venture capital today is effectively an AI bet, and High Vista treats its venture allocation as 'a big arrow in the AI quiver' — a core structural vehicle for expressing a long-dated, high-conviction view that AI will change the world without needing to time individual entries.
thesis🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highventure capitalAIHigh Vista
So what: Treat venture allocation itself as a primary AI-exposure lever within a multi-asset portfolio, not a separate diversifier from an AI thesis.
“most of venture today is basically AI.” ▶ 29:56
Perold argues venture timing is extremely hard to call in real time: the standout 2010-2012 mobile-app vintage (shortly after the iPhone launch) produced outsized returns that were not obvious in advance, and crypto venture returns have similarly 'come and gone' unpredictably, arguing for continuous rather than timed venture exposure.
data_point🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumventure capitalcrypto
So what: Maintain a consistent, non-market-timed venture allocation program rather than trying to tactically enter and exit around perceived cycle peaks and troughs.
“the venture investments made in the 2010 2012 you know when mobile applications were just beginning” ▶ 30:41
High Vista deliberately maintains venture exposure across three archetypes simultaneously: large 'durable' brand-name firms, newly formed spinout firms, and highly sector-specialized VCs, rather than concentrating in one type.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumHigh Vistaventure capital
So what: A diversification framework across VC manager archetypes (durable brand / emerging spinout / sector specialist) that LPs building venture programs can replicate.
Biotech: Capacity-Constrained Small-Cap Specialists 2
High Vista's biotech edge is concentrated in micro- and small-cap biotech equities managed by small, science-literate specialist funds, because these companies are cash-burning, largely revenue-less, and binary (acquired by a pharma company or fail), making them too small for large biotech funds to trade actively — creating a capacity moat for nimble small managers.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: highHigh Vistabiotech
So what: Small-cap biotech is a structurally capacity-constrained niche where large biotech funds are forced into buy-and-hold behavior, leaving room for smaller, agile specialist managers to outperform.
“So the big money in biotech is buy and hold. The small money can come in and move around.” ▶ 12:58
The recurring biotech-manager pattern High Vista exploits is backing brilliant scientist-investors who leave larger biotech funds to start their own small shops, but this creates a recurring 'high-class problem': deciding what to do once that manager's assets under management or opportunity set outgrows the original small-cap capacity edge.
causal🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumHigh Vistabiotech
So what: Anticipate the lifecycle of successful small-cap biotech managers and pre-plan the capacity/exit conversation before AUM growth erodes the original edge.
“there's a large supply of brilliant people at bigger firms who leave to form their own firm and we will back them.” ▶ 14:45
Overlooked Regional and Niche Opportunities 2
Perold sees non-flashy European opportunities as attractive because investor attention is concentrated on the US and AI, citing cross-jurisdictional restructuring situations (companies operating across multiple European legal jurisdictions) and cheap, overlooked quality franchises as areas where a specialist can find real edge.
thesis🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: mediumEurope
So what: European restructuring and overlooked-franchise investing may offer relatively uncrowded value precisely because global capital attention is dominated by the US AI narrative.
“There are a lot of franchises in Europe that are great franchises that people don't seem to care about. It's non AI and they don't seem to care.” ▶ 47:25
High Vista treats tactical niches like Japan equity activism and long/short traded credit as opportunistic plays it dips into within its multi-asset mandate without building permanent dedicated infrastructure, because it expects these niches lack a durable moat and will eventually be competed away.
framework🗣 Andre Peroldconviction: lowJapan activism
So what: Distinguish 'enduring' specialist strategies worth permanent infrastructure from opportunistic tactical trades that should be sized and staffed as temporary, since crowding is expected.
“So, is it a niche that has the moes that these other niches we've talked about have? No. Can others cotton onto it and go and compete away? Yes.” ▶ 46:20

Why OpenAI and Anthropic Won't Win Finance67 min44 insights2026-09-22
▶ Watch on YouTube
Rogo CEO Gabe argues that within 10 years the majority of enterprise value at top investment firms and banks will sit in software/data systems rather than people, and that Rogo can win finance not by out-modeling OpenAI/Anthropic but by building the compliance, data-room, and transaction-plumbing layer the labs will never bother with.
