Geopolitics: Trade Dependence & Conflict Resolution 2
Durable US-China peace is unlikely to come from an algorithmic solution such as privacy-preserving multi-party computation for AI-mediated diplomacy; the actual lever is geopolitical/infrastructural — re-domesticating supply chains globally so no country structurally depends on trade with rivals for critical goods, which lowers the economic cost of decoupling and reduces conflict incentives.
contrarian🗣 Alexconviction: mediumChinasupply chainsmulti-party computationTaiwan
So what: Pushes back on techno-optimist 'AI will solve diplomacy' narratives in favor of a reshoring/supply-chain-independence thesis as the real de-escalation mechanism — relevant to reshoring and critical-minerals investment theses.
“obliterate, cook, incinerate the need for global trade in products and services. And perversely, I think you get a very peaceful world.” ▶ 1:23:40
US fracking-driven energy independence is cited as reducing US strategic exposure to Middle East conflict, offered as the template for how eliminating structural resource/trade dependence (rather than diplomacy) reduces the likelihood and stakes of great-power conflict.
causal🗣 Alexconviction: mediumfrackingMiddle Eastenergy independence
So what: A concrete historical precedent (US shale/fracking) used to argue that critical-resource self-sufficiency is a stronger peace mechanism than diplomatic frameworks — applicable to how AI-era critical inputs (chips, energy, rare earths) get treated strategically.
AI-Driven Cybersecurity: New Attack Surface 4
A healthcare-company CISO reported that a locally-run open-weight model (referenced as a 'Qwen'-class model run with open agentic tooling) under his desk outperforms the paid penetration testers his company hires for roughly $50,000 per engagement, finding vulnerabilities in about 10 minutes that the human firm misses.
anecdote🗣 audience caller (healthcare company CISO)conviction: highQwenpenetration testingcybersecurity
So what: A first-hand, concrete data point that open-weight LLMs have already surpassed professional human penetration testers in speed and coverage — a leading indicator for both offensive AI risk and defensive cybersecurity demand.
“It's the best pen tester I've ever had in my life. I pay these guys 50 grand to find things and I'm not finding them and this thing finds them in 10 minutes.” ▶ 50:21
Given how easily open-weight models can now automate penetration testing and vulnerability discovery, the single best business opportunity right now is forward cyber-defense built to counter AI-discovered vulnerabilities.
thesis🗣 Daveconviction: highcybersecurityopen-weight models
So what: A direct investment thesis call-out: AI-native defensive cybersecurity as 'the business opportunity of the century' given the offense/defense asymmetry created by open-weight LLMs.
“the business opportunity of the century though is the defense... the forward defense against that” ▶ 51:42
Expect a roughly 18-month window of 'vulnerability discovery hell' as AI models systematically surface zero-day vulnerabilities across open-source software and enterprise systems, with the rate of discovery following a bounded (e.g., Gaussian-like) distribution that should peak and then decline once the backlog of latent vulnerabilities is exhausted.
prediction🗣 Alexconviction: mediumzero-day vulnerabilitiesopen-source software
So what: Gives boards/CISOs a specific timeframe (~18 months) and a testable shape (rise-then-decline) for benchmarking and communicating the AI-driven vulnerability surge, useful for cybersecurity budget planning.
Corporate boards face a growing, largely unaddressed legal liability overhang because AI agents deployed inside companies are already taking actions that are 'fairly illegal' in many organizations, prompting a forthcoming paper (co-authored with a veteran public-company board member) on how boards should govern this risk.
data_point🗣 audience member (Michael)conviction: mediumcorporate governanceAI agentsboard liability
So what: Flags an emerging governance/legal-liability risk category (autonomous agent actions inside enterprises) that isn't yet priced into corporate risk frameworks — relevant to GRC/compliance-tech investment.
“AI agents are doing fairly illegal things in a lot of the companies” ▶ 53:40
Applied AI Monetization Playbooks 7
For an autonomous food-service robotics founder, the recommended strategy is to pair the robotics with drone-based delivery to eliminate the real-estate/location cost structure that traditional restaurants pay for Main Street visibility, and to franchise the hardware model first (analogous to how EMC scaled servers) before investing in fully custom proprietary hardware.
framework🗣 Daveconviction: mediumdrone deliveryroboticsfranchising
So what: A concrete go-to-market sequencing playbook for physical-AI/robotics founders: franchise for scale first, then vertically integrate hardware.
When a worker's AI-driven productivity has risen 2-5x but their income hasn't, the root cause is that the employer/shareholders are capturing all of the resulting value; the fix is not asking for a raise but becoming an equity owner in the business.
causal🗣 Daveconviction: highequity compensationproductivity
So what: A direct, generalizable diagnosis for the widening gap between individual AI productivity gains and wage growth, reinforcing the broader equity-over-payroll thesis.
For a long-running Japan-based English-language education company (a market estimated around $10-20 billion), the natural multi-hundred-billion-dollar expansion path is pivoting from 'language learning' into an AI-driven life-coaching platform, because students primarily want social confidence, humor, and fluency validation rather than pure grammatical correctness.

For AI-generated film/video creators, the recommended strategy is to study the Chinese short-video market, which is saturated with AI-generated 'micro-dramas' produced with models like Seedance 2.5, and to release many micro-content pieces to see what the market responds to and iterate, rather than betting everything on one large production — a strategy only feasible in the last two years.

framework🗣 Peterconviction: mediumscreenwritinggenerative AI

Latent Space 1 episode(s) · 38 insights

🔬 Google's AI Scientist Started as an Attempt to Automate Kaggle — John Platt, Google Fellow121 min38 insights2026-09-22
▶ Watch on YouTube
Google Fellow John Platt describes ERA, an internal 'AI scientist' that turns scientific problems into LLM-scored, Monte-Carlo-searched code-mutation tasks, explains why it only became viable around Gemini 2.5, and walks through concrete wins (contrail climate modeling, CDC flu/COVID forecasting) alongside hard warnings about overfitting, plus his views on fusion, quantum computing timelines, and wildfire-detection satellites.
ERA: Google's AI-Scientist Engine · 7Overfitting, Rigor, and the Limits of Automated Discovery · 5Gemini/LLM Capability Trajectory as a Leading Indicator · 3Climate Interventions: Contrails, CO2, and Wildfires · 7Fusion Energy: Economics and Realistic Timeline · 5Quantum Computing: Realistic State of the Art · 3How AI Is Reshaping Scientific Practice and Talent · 5Historical Parallels: Compute-Algorithm Co-evolution · 3
ERA: Google's AI-Scientist Engine 7
ERA works by mapping a scientific problem into a 'scorable task': a Python notebook with a scoring function that Gemini repeatedly mutates and re-scores, turning open-ended science into an optimization loop.
framework🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleERAGeminiKaggle
So what: This is a generalizable product pattern (LLM + scorable-task harness) that could be repackaged for any domain with a measurable objective, not just science.
ERA's internal search algorithm is Monte Carlo tree search using Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) selection, an optimistic reinforcement-learning method that picks the candidate notebook with the highest projected (roughly 95th-percentile) outcome rather than the current best performer, which is why it explores rather than greedily exploits.
framework🗣 John Plattconviction: highERAGeminiUCBMonte Carlo search
So what: Shows the mechanism differentiating ERA from naive agentic code-optimization loops; a reusable design pattern for anyone building agentic search products.
ERA grows about 10 candidate notebooks ('leaves') per iteration as a deliberate compute/learning trade-off: too few parallel branches wastes time, but too many (e.g., 1,000 at once) burns compute without letting branches learn from each other's results.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: mediumERAGemini
So what: A concrete parallelism/cost tuning parameter for anyone building or evaluating similar agentic search infrastructure.
ERA originated internally as the 'AutoKaggle problem' because Kaggle is part of Google, i.e., the project's tree-search, scoring-driven shape comes directly from trying to build a system that could automatically win Kaggle competitions.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleKaggleERA
So what: Useful provenance detail: Google is using its owned Kaggle platform as both training ground and benchmark for its internal agentic-science product line.
“it was called the auto kaggle problem” ▶ 35:19
ERA's capability was gated almost entirely by underlying LLM quality: Platt states the system 'would have been impossible under Gemini 2.0' and only started working around Gemini 2.5, then kept improving with 'every half version' of Gemini since.
causal🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleGeminiERA
So what: Agentic science/coding products are downstream of base-model quality, not just harness engineering — a signal that model releases (not just app-layer work) are the real unlock for this product category.
“I think it would have been impossible under Gemini 2.0.” ▶ 19:58
Google has combined ERA with its 'Antigravity' coding-agent harness in an unpublished preprint, letting the system pull in many research papers and generate code from them; this combined ERA+Antigravity capability is not yet publicly available (Antigravity itself is public).
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: mediumGoogleERAAntigravityGemini
So what: Signals a near-term Google product roadmap item worth tracking: literature-grounded agentic coding for research, beyond today's public Antigravity release.
ERA is explicitly designed for human-in-the-loop use, not one-shot autonomy: it runs for a few hours, returns results, and a human ('almost like a hyper-eager grad student who doesn't sleep') redirects it — Platt contrasts this with other more autonomous agent tools he has tried.
framework🗣 John Plattconviction: highERAGemini
So what: Positions ERA's UX/workflow (periodic human check-ins vs. fire-and-forget) as a deliberate design choice, relevant to how enterprises should structure human oversight of agentic research tools.
Overfitting, Rigor, and the Limits of Automated Discovery 5
Platt distinguishes 'predictive models' (lowest error on a dataset) from 'descriptive models' (capture the underlying mechanism and can extrapolate, like Newton's gravity vs. apples); ERA today mostly builds predictive/descriptive-adjacent models using 'LLM intuition' rather than discovering genuinely new physics from scratch.
framework🗣 John Plattconviction: highERAGemini
So what: A useful due-diligence lens for evaluating any 'AI for science' claim: ask whether the output is a fitted statistical model or a genuinely extrapolative mechanistic one.
Platt calls ERA 'a power tool' that 'can slice your fingers off': because it relentlessly runs effectively unlimited hypothesis tests, using it requires far more rigor (hidden hold-out sets, careful validation) than traditional manual research to avoid catastrophic overfitting.
contrarian🗣 John Plattconviction: highERAGemini
So what: A direct risk warning for any team adopting agentic research/optimization tools at scale: automation increases overfitting risk, it doesn't reduce the need for statistical discipline.
≈ “It's a power tool. It can slice your fingers off.” paraphrase
Platt explicitly invokes Goodhart's Law — 'any metric that becomes a target is no longer good as a metric' — and says it applies individually to every scoring function/leaderboard built for agentic systems, meaning scoring functions must be continually revised as the AI finds exploits.
framework🗣 John Plattconviction: highERAKaggle
So what: Operational implication: any org deploying scorable-task agents needs an ongoing process to detect and patch metric-gaming, not a one-time spec.
“any metric that becomes a target is no longer good as a metric” ▶ 36:52
In a Google-run contrail Kaggle competition, human competitors beat Google's own model by exploiting a half-pixel labeling ambiguity (whether pixel coordinate 0,0 was the corner or center), showing that reward-hacking/overfitting behavior is not unique to AI agents — skilled humans do it too.
anecdote🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleKaggle
So what: Illustrates that leaderboard/competition-based benchmarks (a common way AI progress is marketed) are vulnerable to label-artifact exploitation, a caution for interpreting benchmark claims generally.
Platt argues 'taste' (creative scientific judgment) and 'rigor' (validation discipline) are the two enduring human skills that AI-for-science tools do not replace, and that they don't necessarily need to reside in the same person — he sees this pattern extending beyond science into software engineering generally.
framework🗣 John Plattconviction: mediumGoogleERA
So what: A hiring/org-design implication as coding and analysis get automated: value concentrates in judgment (taste) and verification (rigor) roles rather than raw code production.
Gemini/LLM Capability Trajectory as a Leading Indicator 3
Platt says there has been a 'phase change' in AI-for-science tooling in just the last 12-18 months, driven by general-purpose LLMs (Gemini) replacing the previous approach of building bespoke classical ML models (CNNs, etc.) for each individual scientific problem.
thesis🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleGemini
So what: Confirms a broader capability inflection point around 2024-2025 that is showing up concretely in scientific R&D productivity, not just chat/coding benchmarks.
Despite large coding and knowledge-synthesis gains, Platt still sees a persistent 'jagged frontier' in Gemini: strong at coding, gathering knowledge, and finding related work, but not yet showing comparable gains in scientific rigor, philosophy, or genuinely new creative hypothesis generation.
contrarian🗣 John Plattconviction: mediumGoogleGemini
So what: Suggests near-term monetizable value from frontier models is concentrated in acceleration/productivity tooling rather than autonomous scientific discovery — relevant to calibrating AI-for-science market hype.
≈ “I'm a big believer in sort of the whole jagged frontier.” paraphrase
Platt recalls asking Gemini 2.5 to write boosted-decision-tree code the day it launched and it 'just worked,' which he treated personally as a signal of 'a new world' in model capability — an example of a qualitative jump he uses as a heuristic for judging how significant a model release actually is.
anecdote🗣 John Plattconviction: mediumGoogleGemini
So what: A practical evaluator's heuristic: test a frontier model on a well-known, moderately hard implementation task on release day as a quick signal of real capability jumps versus marketing.
Climate Interventions: Contrails, CO2, and Wildfires 7
Contrails are estimated to cause about 1% of all anthropogenic global warming; in high-air-traffic regions like Europe, persistent contrail cirrus adds roughly 1 watt/m² of local radiative forcing, versus about 3 watts/m² for average global anthropogenic forcing from all sources combined.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleclimateaviation
So what: Quantifies a specific, addressable slice of aviation's climate impact that is disproportionately large relative to its cost of mitigation.
Avoiding ice-supersaturated atmospheric regions (which cause persistent, warming contrails) costs airlines only a minor fuel penalty — dropping about two flight levels — making contrail avoidance one of the cheapest, highest-leverage climate interventions Google's team has identified; unlike CO2, the benefit is local, so a single country acting alone captures most of the gain.
thesis🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleaviationclimate
So what: A rare 'cheap and unilateral' climate lever — relevant to aviation regulators, airlines, and climate-tech investors looking for low-capex, high-impact interventions.
ERA solved a climate-modeling problem Google's own scientists had been stuck on for two years — building a counterfactual model to estimate the sunlight-reflection cooling effect of contrails — by finding a new, surprisingly simple combination of confounding variables the humans hadn't tried, succeeding where the team's own manual attempts had failed their own synthetic-data validation tests.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleERAGeminiclimate
So what: Concrete evidence that agentic search tools can outperform expert humans on well-defined but confounder-rich statistical modeling problems, even without needing 'big' models.
The uncertainty in how much atmospheric CO2 the biosphere will absorb by 2100 spans roughly ±300 ppm, against a current atmospheric level of about 440-450 ppm — an enormous unresolved range that Google's satellite-data-fusion work on CO2 estimation (combining sparse high-accuracy OCO-2/OCO-3 measurements with frequent, lower-resolution weather-satellite infrared data) aims to narrow.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleOCO-2OCO-3climateCO2
So what: Highlights a major, underappreciated source of long-run climate-forecast uncertainty that better remote-sensing/AI fusion models could materially reduce.
Google, via the nonprofit Earth Fire Alliance and satellite maker Muon Space, has launched a prototype for a planned constellation of roughly 50-80 low-earth-orbit mid-wave-infrared satellites designed to detect wildfires as small as about 5 meters across, anywhere on Earth, within 15-20 minutes, enabling intervention while fires are still cheap to extinguish.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleEarth Fire AllianceMuon Spacewildfire detection
So what: A concrete space-tech/climate-resilience infrastructure project with named commercial partners (Muon Space) worth tracking for procurement, partnership, or investment signals.
The World Health Organization estimates about 300,000 excess deaths per year globally from wildfire smoke, which Platt cites as the core justification for treating early wildfire detection as a direct public-health investment, not just a property-protection or climate-mitigation project.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: highWorld Health Organizationwildfire detectionGoogle
So what: Reframes wildfire early-warning infrastructure as a public-health ROI case, which could support different funding/regulatory channels than pure climate-mitigation framing.
AI has produced a major step-change in weather forecasting (up to ~15 days out, including notably improved tropical cyclone track/intensification prediction), but climate modeling (multi-decade) remains largely unsolved by AI because it is fundamentally a low-data, non-stationary problem — Platt states plainly that 'AI has not revolutionized' climate modeling the way it has weather.
contrarian🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleclimate modelingweather forecasting
So what: A clear expectation-setting signal for climate-tech and reinsurance markets: don't extrapolate AI's weather-forecasting wins onto long-horizon climate projections — the data regime is fundamentally different.
Fusion Energy: Economics and Realistic Timeline 5
Platt states there is a 'definite probability' of commercially relevant fusion power by the end of this decade, revising the old 'fusion is always 30 years away' joke down to roughly three years in his personal estimate.
prediction🗣 John Plattconviction: mediumfusion energyTAE Technologies
So what: A directly actionable timeline view from a Google scientist who has worked hands-on with a fusion startup (TAE), useful for calibrating fusion-sector investment horizons.
≈ “I think it's like three years away, not 30 years away.” paraphrase
Fusion viability reduces to the Lawson criterion: the product of plasma density, temperature, and confinement time must exceed a fixed threshold, and every fusion approach has an 'Achilles heel' where one of the three numbers lags; Platt warns readers to be skeptical of 'breathless' fusion news that touts progress on only one of the three metrics.
framework🗣 John Plattconviction: highfusion energyLawson criterion
So what: A concrete due-diligence checklist for evaluating fusion-startup progress claims: ask which of the three Lawson-criterion variables actually improved, and whether the other two held.
“you have to be a little bit cautious about the breathless news articles that only talk about one number” ▶ 1:17:24
Fully electrifying the economy to cut emissions would require roughly 5x today's electricity generation, and pushing renewables-plus-battery systems to cover the 'last 20%' of load becomes prohibitively expensive due to the sheer number of batteries needed — implying a real economic case for baseload alternatives like fusion, not just a decarbonization nice-to-have.
framework🗣 John Plattconviction: highfusion energyrenewable energybattery storage
So what: Supports the investment thesis for baseload/dispatchable clean power (fusion, advanced nuclear) as complementary to, not competitive with, renewables plus storage.
A startup reportedly attempted transmuting mercury into gold using neutron flux from a fusion reactor as a monetization strategy, illustrating that some fusion ventures are pursuing creative non-energy revenue streams as a bridge before reactors are cost-competitive on power generation alone.
anecdote🗣 John Plattconviction: lowfusion energy
So what: A signal of capital-efficiency pressure in the fusion sector — investors should watch for similar 'byproduct monetization' business models emerging among fusion startups.
Google DeepMind has developed AI-based control systems for tokamak fusion reactors specifically to prevent disruptions (where the plasma suddenly dumps all its confined energy into one point, damaging the vacuum vessel), representing a distinct AI application in fusion engineering separate from ERA-style scientific modeling.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogle DeepMindfusion energytokamak
So what: Identifies a specific, already-deployed AI application (plasma control) within fusion engineering that reduces catastrophic hardware-failure risk, relevant to assessing fusion-reactor capex risk.
Quantum Computing: Realistic State of the Art 3
Platt assesses quantum computing as 'not 30 years away' but also 'not tomorrow,' estimating a few to several years before Google's roadmap-driven scaling effort reaches a milestone enabling genuinely useful quantum computation; because current systems are fundamentally analog and 'finicky,' he does not expect a sudden phase change even from a hardware breakthrough.
prediction🗣 John Plattconviction: mediumGooglequantum computing
So what: A grounded timeline from a Google insider co-authoring on the quantum team's work, useful for calibrating expectations around near-term quantum-computing commercialization claims.
Current superconducting quantum systems remain limited to on the order of 100-200 qubits, with the physical-to-logical qubit ratio still too high for practical error correction; the core bottleneck is the 2D chip connectivity used to wire superconducting qubits together, while alternative modalities like neutral atoms theoretically allow denser connectivity but remain far less proven at scale.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: mediumGooglequantum computingsuperconducting qubitsneutral atoms
So what: A concrete technical bottleneck (2D connectivity, qubit error-correction ratio) to track as the key leading indicator of near-term quantum progress across competing hardware modalities.
Google's 'quantum echoes' algorithm, which Platt co-authored, is a practical near-term (pre-fault-tolerant) application: it fits a parameterized physical Hamiltonian to observed experimental data (e.g., decoding NMR parameters), meaning useful quantum-computing applications can exist before full error-corrected quantum advantage is achieved.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: mediumGooglequantum computingNMR
So what: Identifies a specific, already-published near-term quantum use case (physical parameter fitting) that doesn't require waiting for large-scale fault tolerance — relevant for scientific/industrial early adopters.
How AI Is Reshaping Scientific Practice and Talent 5
ERA-style tools are shifting scientists' time away from data engineering and grunt coding toward defining the right scoring/cost function, which Platt describes as 'almost the essence of the scientific problem' — the human skill in demand is now specifying what to optimize, not writing the code to optimize it.
thesis🗣 John Plattconviction: highERAGoogle
So what: Reframes the highest-value human skill in AI-augmented science/engineering as problem specification and objective design, a transferable insight for any org automating technical work with agents.
Google's ERA system won the CDC's competition to forecast next week's COVID and flu case counts by US state and territory — notable because Google originally pioneered disease-tracking-via-search-data two decades earlier with Google Flu Trends, so Platt frames this as the field coming full circle.
data_point🗣 John Plattconviction: highGoogleERACDCGoogle Flu Trends
So what: A concrete, verifiable win (beating a real public-health forecasting competition) that demonstrates ERA's real-world applicability beyond internal research demos.
Platt's career advice for young scientists is to keep building deep domain expertise while aggressively experimenting with AI tools in parallel, citing his own 21-year-old son — studying RNA biology while also using vibe-coding tools — as the model he considers correct for the current environment.
framework🗣 John Plattconviction: mediumGoogle
So what: A practical talent-development heuristic for hiring/training in technical fields during rapid AI capability growth: domain depth plus tool fluency, not either alone.
Physical lab experimentation remains a hard, unsolved bottleneck for AI-driven science because tools like ERA are 'all computational' and still depend on humans to gather real-world data; Platt's explicit wish, if he could remove one industry bottleneck, is a generalized automated lab that could execute arbitrary physical experiments from a JSON specification — which he says would require solving an 'AI-complete robotics problem.'
prediction🗣 John Plattconviction: highERAroboticsGoogle
So what: Identifies automated/robotic physical experimentation ('self-driving labs') as the single highest-leverage unmet infrastructure gap for compounding AI-driven scientific progress — a clear investable thesis area.
“someone please make the the everything lab that you could like send JSON blob to and it will do any experiment at all” ▶ 2:00:24
Platt argues that multiple-hypothesis-testing risk (false discovery) is structurally worse with agentic tools like ERA than with manual research because the agent effectively runs an unbounded number of hypothesis tests ('a billion darts'), which is exactly the Kaggle-style overfitting problem the project was originally built to confront.
causal🗣 John Plattconviction: highERAKaggle
So what: A methodological warning applicable to any quant/research team adopting LLM-driven automated hypothesis search: statistical significance thresholds and hold-out discipline need to be tightened, not loosened, as automation scales.
Historical Parallels: Compute-Algorithm Co-evolution 3
Platt argues 1980s neural-network research (Hopfield networks, which he studied directly under Nobel laureate John Hopfield at Caltech) generated similar hype to today's AI ('this has the capability of revolutionizing computing') but stalled for roughly two decades purely due to lack of compute, not flawed ideas — in his words, 'the hopes and dreams completely outstripped the capabilities because we effectively had zero compute.'
thesis🗣 John Plattconviction: highJohn HopfieldCaltechNeurIPSneural networks
So what: A calibration framework for judging current AI hype cycles: distinguish whether a stalled idea is conceptually wrong or merely compute-starved, since compute-starved ideas can resurface and succeed decades later.
≈ “the hopes and dreams completely outstripped the capabilities because we effectively had zero compute” paraphrase
Platt states that GPUs surpassing CPUs in raw compute speed happened around 2007-2012, coinciding closely with (and enabling) landmark deep-learning breakthroughs like the original ImageNet results and early speech-recognition systems, and that this timing 'is not a coincidence.'
causal🗣 John Plattconviction: highGPUCPUImageNetdeep learning
So what: Reinforces that hardware-algorithm co-evolution, not algorithmic insight alone, drives AI capability step-changes — relevant for forecasting when today's compute bottlenecks (e.g., inference cost, memory bandwidth) might similarly unlock the next capability jump.
Platt recounts a secondhand story that a Caltech grad-school friend named Brian (an early NVIDIA employee working on CUDA/BLAS for deep learning) convinced Jensen Huang in a roughly 15-minute conversation to pivot NVIDIA's strategic focus from gaming toward general-purpose GPU compute for deep learning, after which the company shifted direction essentially overnight.
anecdote🗣 John Plattconviction: lowNvidiaJensen HuangCUDA
So what: A secondhand but notable origin-story data point on how NVIDIA's pivot into AI compute (now central to its market value) reportedly traces to a single internal conversation rather than a top-down strategic study — useful color for understanding platform-shift decision-making at scale.

What Bitcoin Did · Peter McCormack 1 episode(s) · 38 insights

The Greatest Fraud In Human History | Prof. Richard Werner67 min38 insights2026-09-22
▶ Watch on YouTube
Economist Richard Werner argues mainstream economics has deliberately obscured banks' power to create money, that central bankers act as 'traitors' who use engineered crises to concentrate global ownership and power, and that the decades-long shift toward a handful of giant banks in the UK/US (versus thousands of small local lenders in high-growth Japan/China/pre-WWI Britain) directly explains stagnant productivity, boom-bust cycles, and the removal of reformist politicians like Liz Truss.
Money Creation & Concealment Mechanics · 6Central Bank Power, 'Traitor' Thesis & Engineered Crises · 5Credit-Driven Growth: Japan and China's Playbook · 6UK Banking Concentration, the Productivity Puzzle & Community Banking · 6Monetary Policy Critique: Interest Rates as 'Bait' · 4Liz Truss, the 2022 Gilt Crisis & Central Bank-Media Relations · 3EU/ECB Centralization Risk & Capital Controls · 3Political Realignment: AfD, German Sovereignty & UK Institutions · 5
Money Creation & Concealment Mechanics 6
Banks create new money at the moment they issue a loan rather than lending out pre-existing deposits, and this was only empirically proven for the first time in 2014 (by Werner's own study), despite being treated as a 'conspiracy theory' for decades.
data_point🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highbanking systemfinancial intermediation theory
So what: If true, it undermines the standard 'loanable funds' model used across finance and macro forecasting; investors relying on textbook fractional-reserve logic may be mispricing how credit expansion actually works.
≈ “The first empirical study was only conducted in 2014... And the claim that, well, banks create money when they lend was considered a conspiracy theory.” paraphrase ▶ 13:01
Paul Samuelson's 'partial reservation' (fractional reserve) theory, popularized in his 1947 economics textbook and its successive editions, is a half-truth that obscured the fact that individual banks create money, rather than merely intermediating deposits.
framework🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highPaul Samuelson
So what: Explains why generations of economists, policymakers and financial journalists default to a deposit-intermediation mental model that Werner claims materially misdescribes how credit and money supply actually expand.
Double-entry bookkeeping, invented roughly 800 years ago by and for bankers, functions as a concealment device because balanced asset-liability ledgers make bank money creation invisible even to trained economists.
causal🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highdouble-entry bookkeeping
So what: A reminder that standard accounting conventions can structurally hide the mechanics of credit creation from analysts who only read balance sheets at face value.
“All accounts always balance... show me where the money creation is?” ▶ 14:23
Judge central bankers and central planners by their actions rather than their stated justifications ('revealed preferences'), since their public explanations are designed to mislead.
framework🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highcentral banks
So what: A usable heuristic for reading central-bank communications: weight policy actions and outcomes over forward guidance and stated rationale when forecasting future moves.
“Don't listen to what they say... Just watch what they do.” ▶ 4:47
Werner's open-access papers 'Can Banks Individually Create Money Out of Nothing?' and 'The Lost Century in Economics' lay out the empirical case that banks create money and that mainstream economics denied this for roughly a century.
data_point🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highRichard Werner
So what: Primary sources an investor could read directly to evaluate Werner's credit-creation thesis rather than relying on the interview summary alone.
Mainstream economics since Ricardo uses a 'deductive' method that starts from a desired conclusion and reverse-engineers assumptions and models to reach it, and every major school (classical, Keynesian, neoclassical, monetarist) rests on an unproven equilibrium assumption (supply always equals demand).
framework🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highDavid RicardoKeynesian economics
So what: A methodological red flag for evaluating any economic model or forecast that leans on equilibrium assumptions without empirical validation.
“There was never any evidence of any equilibrium, was there? So, this is all a whole bunch of nonsense.” ▶ 18:43
Central Bank Power, 'Traitor' Thesis & Engineered Crises 5
Central bankers in most countries function as 'traitors' who serve a globalist ownership-concentration agenda rather than their own nation's economic interests.
thesis🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highcentral banks
So what: Sets the interpretive frame for treating central bank crisis responses as potentially serving foreign/ownership-transfer objectives rather than domestic stabilization, worth stress-testing against specific policy episodes.
“In most countries, central bankers turn out to be, essentially, traitors to their countries who, when it comes down to it, work for other interests that seem to be... too globalist.” ▶ 0:01
A World Bank report line stating that 'banking crises are a window of opportunity' to change ownership structure is cited as evidence that crises (e.g., the 1997-98 Asian Financial Crisis) are used deliberately to transfer previously locally-owned banks and economies into foreign ownership.
causal🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highWorld BankAsian Financial Crisis
So what: A lens for investors to re-examine future 'crisis' periods for unusual patterns of foreign/institutional ownership transfer in the affected sector, not just price dislocation.
“As the World Bank once said in its report: Banking crises... are a window of opportunity.” ▶ 0:16
The creation of a central bank has historically coincided with the introduction of new sovereign-level taxes (e.g., the US federal income tax was created alongside the Federal Reserve in 1913), because the interest-bearing national debt the central bank enables requires a new government revenue stream to service.
causal🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highFederal Reserve
So what: A historical pattern to watch for in any country introducing or restructuring a central bank: expect accompanying tax-base expansion to fund newly created public debt.
Japanese central bank officials, before taking leadership positions, reportedly stated on the record (quoted in Nikkei) their intent to pursue 'structural transformation' and change Japan's ownership structure -- evidence, in Werner's view, that the 1990s bubble collapse and subsequent 'lost decades' recession were deliberately engineered rather than accidental policy failures.
contrarian🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highBank of JapanJapan
So what: Reframes a canonical 'accidental bubble-and-bust' case study as an intentional policy outcome -- relevant precedent for assessing whether current central-bank tightening cycles could similarly be structural-transformation tools rather than purely anti-inflation measures.
“We think it's better for the economy to have structural changes... structural transformation... we want to change the ownership structure.” ▶ 7:59
Werner's power-progression framework: once a network controls 'all the money in the world,' its next objective becomes concentrating political power globally into a small number of hands, i.e., a centralized world government.
thesis🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: mediumcentral banks
So what: A high-level geopolitical prior that frames monetary and banking-consolidation policy as instrumental to a broader power-centralization goal, useful context (though speculative) for interpreting supranational financial regulation.
“First it's about money, but once you have all the money in the world... the next step is power.” ▶ 15:04
Credit-Driven Growth: Japan and China's Playbook 6
Postwar Japan achieved roughly 15% sustained annual GDP growth for decades as a deliberate policy choice; PM Ikeda's 1960 pledge to double national income within 10 years was actually achieved in 4.5 years, despite economist ridicule at the time.
data_point🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highJapanIkeda
So what: Used as the empirical anchor for Werner's claim that double-digit sustained growth is achievable via policy choice (credit allocation), not a historical fluke -- a benchmark for evaluating any country's growth-policy ambitions.
“They did it in 4 and a half years, you know, way ahead of schedule.” ▶ 9:43
Core causal model: economic growth is 'a directly proportional function of bank credit creation' for productive business investment, not of interest-rate policy or fiscal stimulus.
framework🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highbank credit
So what: A concrete, testable macro framework: an investor tracking bank lending-to-business growth rates (vs. lending to consumption or asset purchases) gets a leading indicator Werner claims outperforms rate-based models for forecasting GDP growth.
≈ “What drives growth in a very specific way, economic growth is simply a directly proportional function of bank credit creation for productive business investments.” paraphrase
China's 1978 reform program traces directly to Deng Xiaoping sending 300 officials to Japan to learn its growth model before implementing bank-credit-led industrial policy at home.
anecdote🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highDeng XiaopingChinaJapan
So what: Positions China's growth model as a direct, deliberate transplant of the Japanese credit-allocation playbook rather than a sui generis 'socialist market economy' innovation -- relevant for forecasting how China might respond to future growth slowdowns (reverting to credit-expansion tools).
“Deng Xiaoping came to power, and the first thing he did was, 'Let's go to Japan.' He went there with 300 of his leading men.” ▶ 20:45
The Japan/China growth prescription centers on building thousands of small local banks, each with many branches and loan officers making localized small-business lending decisions, replacing a single centralized bank/state lender.
framework🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highChinaJapansmall banks
So what: A structural, testable growth lever for policymakers or investors evaluating emerging-market banking reform: watch bank-count and local-lending decentralization trends as a growth signal, not just headline credit growth.
“How about creating 5,000 banks, and each bank would have 30 branches, and each branch would have 35 loan officers?” ▶ 34:12
Small and medium firms account for roughly 65% of UK jobs and historically 70-80% of jobs in Japan and Germany, so their access (or lack of access) to bank credit is the primary determinant of national productivity.
data_point🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highUKGermanyJapansmall and medium enterprises
So what: Gives investors a concrete metric to track (small-business lending volumes/approval rates) as a productivity leading indicator across these economies.
“Small and medium-sized companies typically account for two-thirds of jobs. In the UK, this figure is approximately 65%.” ▶ 24:58
The type of bank credit created determines the macro outcome: credit to productive business investment produces growth without inflation; credit to consumption produces CPI inflation; credit to asset purchases (real estate, and today largely via big-bank lending to private-equity and hedge funds) produces asset-price inflation and banking crises -- Werner's explanation for the UK's repeated boom-bust cycle over the last century.
causal🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highprivate equityhedge fundsUK
So what: A disaggregated-credit framework for forecasting inflation type and asset-bubble risk: rising bank lending specifically to PE/hedge funds and real estate is Werner's flagged precursor to asset-price crises, distinct from consumer-credit-driven CPI inflation.
“When banks lend for productive business investments, you get growth, no inflation, and no crises... when banks lend to buy assets... you have asset inflation and a banking crisis.” ▶ 45:15
UK Banking Concentration, the Productivity Puzzle & Community Banking 6
The UK's 'big five' banks have balance sheets over £2 trillion and control over 80% of deposits, and no longer do traditional small-business lending, instead prioritizing large deals with big clients, private equity funds and hedge funds.
data_point🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highUKbig five banks
So what: Quantifies the concentration level behind Werner's productivity-puzzle causal claim, useful as a benchmark when comparing UK banking structure to more decentralized systems like Germany's.
≈ “We have five banks... that accounts for over 80% of deposits.” paraphrase ▶ 22:22
The UK's 1918 parliamentary Colvin Report already diagnosed that a concentrated big-bank system starves small firms of long-term credit, and more than a century later the same problem persists unaddressed, which Werner treats as evidence of deliberate policy inertia rather than ignorance.
contrarian🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highUKColvin Report
So what: A century-long documented paper trail investors/policy-watchers can cite as evidence that UK small-business credit starvation is a structural, politically entrenched feature rather than an oversight likely to be reformed soon.
“Over a century ago the problem was identified, but what was done about it? Of course, nothing to this day, because the real goal is concentration.” ▶ 27:55
In the UK, obtaining a banking license for a bank serving the super-rich moves quickly, while attempts to start a local community bank face deliberate, sustained obstruction.
contrarian🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highUKBank of England
So what: A regulatory-asymmetry signal for anyone considering UK challenger/community banking ventures: expect licensing friction disproportionate to risk profile if targeting small-business/local lending.
“If you want to start a new bank for the super-rich... you'll get a banking license in record time... trying to create a local community bank will certainly be challenging.” ▶ 28:25
Werner's own attempt to launch Hampshire Community Bank completed the full Bank of England licensing process (after the BoE repeatedly changed its requirements), but collapsed at the final stage when the largest shareholder withdrew, having funded only part of a promised £5 million commitment.
anecdote🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highHampshire Community BankBank of England
So what: A first-hand cautionary case study on execution risk in UK challenger-bank formation: regulatory approval is achievable, but funding commitments can still collapse late-stage, a specific risk to underwrite in similar ventures.
“The largest shareholder, when we were ready, everything was ready... decided to pull out... they didn't even invest their entire promised 5 million.” ▶ 46:14
Werner's quantified small-bank-formation thesis: a new local bank capitalized with about £20 million in equity can, via lending multiplier effects, generate roughly £400-500 million in loans within 3-4 years, scaling toward £1 billion.
prediction🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highsmall banksUK
So what: A specific, actionable capital-efficiency claim for anyone evaluating community/challenger bank economics -- a testable multiplier ratio (roughly 20-50x equity-to-loan-book over several years) worth diligence before investing in such a venture.
“You only need 20 million capital... you'll soon get a billion loans out of that 20 million capital. This will become the flywheel.” ▶ 44:44
The US bank count fell from roughly 30,000 to about 5,000, and crypto-friendly banks Silvergate and Silicon Valley Bank were 'suddenly destroyed' in 2023 in what Werner and the host characterize as an 'orchestrated operation.'
contrarian🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: mediumSilvergateSilicon Valley Bankcryptocurrency
So what: A contrarian alternative narrative to the standard 'poor risk management' explanation for the 2023 US regional bank failures -- worth flagging as an unverified claim to track if similar crypto-bank-adjacent failures recur.
“All of these banks were interested in cryptocurrency and were active there. And they were suddenly destroyed in what appears to be an orchestrated operation.” ▶ 23:02
Monetary Policy Critique: Interest Rates as 'Bait' 4
Contrary to the central-bank narrative that interest rates are the primary policy tool, Werner's research ('Rethinking Monetary Policy') finds rates largely follow economic growth rather than drive it, making rate-setting essentially 'bait' that distracts from the real lever: the quantity and allocation of bank credit.
contrarian🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highinterest ratescentral banks
So what: Challenges the standard investor framework of positioning around central-bank rate decisions as the primary growth driver; suggests credit-growth/allocation data may be a more reliable forward indicator than the policy rate itself.
≈ “Interest rates, short-term rates... are largely driven by central banks, they follow economic growth... this is essentially, um, bait.” paraphrase ▶ 51:09
Former Bank of England Monetary Policy Committee member Dame Kate Barker publicly stated that her time on the MPC 'was a real waste of time,' which Werner cites as insider confirmation that rate-setting has limited real economic effect.
data_point🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highBank of EnglandKate Barker
So what: A rare on-record insider admission that supports skepticism toward the market's heavy focus on central-bank rate decisions as the dominant growth/inflation driver.
“When I think about my time on the Monetary Policy Committee... it was a real waste of time.” ▶ 51:32
Werner's empirical claim, based on his data analysis, is that interest rates are positively correlated with growth (higher growth leads to higher rates, lower growth to lower rates) -- the reverse of the mainstream causal claim that low rates cause high growth.
data_point🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highinterest rates
So what: If accurate, this inverts a foundational assumption behind rate-cut-driven bullish equity/credit narratives, implying rate cuts may be a lagging symptom of weak growth rather than a forward stimulant.
“Higher growth leads to higher rates, lower growth leads to lower rates, and that's it.” ▶ 51:58
Werner endorses the Hendry 'general-to-specific' econometric method (start with a maximal model containing all candidate explanatory variables, including opponents' preferred ones, then iteratively remove the least significant variable) as a transparent way to let data reveal true causal drivers of growth, inflation and banking crises, contrasting it with both the Austrian school's rejection of econometrics and mainstream deductive (Ricardian) model-building.
framework🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highDavid HendryAustrian economics
So what: A specific, replicable methodology an analyst could apply when testing competing macro hypotheses (e.g., credit growth vs. rates vs. fiscal variables) for driving GDP, inflation or crisis probability.
“You start with... a grand general model, and then you run it, and then you simplify it using a very transparent procedure, removing the least significant variable.” ▶ 53:27
Liz Truss, the 2022 Gilt Crisis & Central Bank-Media Relations 3
Werner frames the September-October 2022 UK gilt-market turmoil that ended Liz Truss's premiership as the Bank of England deliberately selling government bonds to spike yields and manufacture a crisis narrative, which he calls a 'coup d'etat.'
contrarian🗣 Richard Wernerconviction: highBank of EnglandLiz Truss

a16z Just Launched a School With OpenAI, NVIDIA & Anthropic79 min36 insights2026-09-22
▶ Watch on YouTube
Gagan Biyani (Udemy/Sprig/Maven co-founder) launches Horowitz Andreessen Academy, a for-profit, unaccredited post-high-school 'school' in San Francisco backed by $40M from a16z, Ben Horowitz, Marc Andreessen and angels (D'Angelo, Lutke, Xu, Simo, Sankar), with founding partners Anthropic, OpenAI, Nvidia, Coinbase, Meta, Palantir, Replit and Stripe providing compute, curriculum input and hiring pipelines for young 'AI-native builders.'
Academy business model and funding · 5Founding corporate partners as a moat · 4Why delay founding: contrasted with YC, Thiel Fellowship, a16z Speedrun · 4Curriculum design and selection mechanics · 6AI-in-education stance: doomerism vs. optimism · 7Career/talent-market signal: high agency over pedigree · 5Founder biography, failure, and culture · 5
Academy business model and funding 5
Horowitz Andreessen Academy raised $40M with a multi-year runway (not the usual 18-month startup cycle) specifically because a school needs to credibly promise students it won't run out of money before they graduate.
framework🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen AcademyAndreessen Horowitz
So what: Signals a16z is treating education as a long-duration infrastructure bet rather than a typical fast-iterate startup, implying patient capital and a longer payback horizon for investors.
“Instead of raising for 18 months, we raised for a few years. This was very intentional because there's a big difference between starting a school and starting a uh you know AI app.” ▶ 12:17
The academy's angel investor base includes Adam D'Angelo (Quora), Toby Lutke (Shopify), Tony Xu (DoorDash), Fidji Simo, and Shyam Sankar (Palantir), alongside a16z as the lead institutional backer.
data_point🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highAdam D'AngeloToby LutkeTony XuFidji SimoShyam SankarPalantirHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: This roster of operator-angels doubles as a hiring/advisory network and signals how top tech executives view education-for-builders as a category worth personally backing.
The academy is structured for three revenue streams: student tuition (expected to be the bulk of revenue), corporate partnership fees from companies wanting access to top young talent, and eventual equity upside from students' companies.
thesis🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: mediumHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: A school monetizing via corporate access fees and founder equity is a novel EdTech business model worth tracking as a template other 'builder academies' may copy.
The first cohort is a free, one-year 'Founding Class Fellowship' starting September 2027 (students pay only room and board); a full two-year program is planned for 2028, pending regulatory approval, with tuition set at or above elite private university levels.
data_point🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: The pricing strategy (free pilot cohort to build brand/proof points, then premium pricing matching Ivy-tier tuition) is a classic freemium-to-premium playbook applied to education.
The academy is a for-profit startup (not a nonprofit) that is explicitly designed to reach operating profitability, and it is unaccredited, meaning it must still obtain separate regulatory approval before launching paid programs.
data_point🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: Regulatory approval is a real dependency/risk for the 2028 paid two-year program launch and a milestone to watch.
Founding corporate partners as a moat 4
Founding partners of the academy include Anthropic, Coinbase, Meta, Nvidia, OpenAI, Palantir, Replit and Stripe, who commit to curricular input, an advisory group giving feedback on market talent needs, and compute credits for student projects.
data_point🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highAnthropicCoinbaseMetaNvidiaOpenAIPalantirReplitStripeHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: A frontier-lab-and-hyperscaler consortium feeding curriculum and compute into one feeder school could become a fast-track pipeline into these companies' hiring and product ecosystems.
Nvidia's role as a core partner is framed as giving students direct access to the company's latest thinking on model development and the future of AI, something Biyani argues top universities can no longer offer because leading AI researchers now work at companies, not on campuses.
causal🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highNvidiaHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: Reinforces the broader thesis that frontier AI research talent and knowledge have migrated from academia to private labs, favoring corporate-partnered education models over traditional universities.
“It's also interesting that the leading researchers in AI are no longer at university campuses, they are at these companies.” ▶ 11:22

Biyani expects corporate partnerships to become a meaningful, growing revenue stream over time because companies want privileged access to what they believe will be the best young builder talent pool on the planet.
thesis🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: mediumHorowitz Andreessen Academy

Why delay founding: contrasted with YC, Thiel Fellowship, a16z Speedrun 4
Biyani believes only a very small percentage of young builders should start companies immediately after high school; he sees VCs handing out $150k-$250k checks 'on a flyer' to unproven teenage builders as a risky, often premature pattern.
contrarian🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen Academy

“Personally, uh, I believe that only a very very very small percentage of these builders should be starting companies right away.” ▶ 20:18
The academy's philosophy is that students should treat fundraising as always available to them, and therefore should be more selective about taking capital rather than raising just because money is on offer, since startups are '10-year slogs' that require real conviction.
framework🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: A discipline framework for evaluating young/first-time founders: conviction and idea-market fit should precede capital-raising, a useful screen for angels evaluating teenage founders.
“You shouldn't just take money just because it's there.” ▶ 22:05
Unlike the Thiel Fellowship, a16z Speedrun, or YC (which are built around students who are already starting a company), the Horowitz Andreessen Academy is explicitly designed to preserve 1-2 years of unstructured exploration before a student commits to founding or joining a company.
thesis🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highThiel Fellowshipa16z SpeedrunHorowitz Andreessen Academy

Curriculum design and selection mechanics 6
The curriculum reserves 75-80% of student time for self-directed project work rather than classes; classes are kept 'didactic only' and projects are deliberately indirect (not spec'd by the school) so students must exercise real agency rather than fill in a templated assignment.
framework🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: A pedagogical design choice that other 'builder schools' and corporate AI-upskilling programs could copy: minimize structured instruction, maximize ambiguous, self-directed execution.
Student work is organized into three project types: research projects, learning projects, and building/entrepreneurial projects, often combined (e.g. touring manufacturing facilities in Shenzhen while studying robotics online and building a physical robot).
framework🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: Illustrates the academy's bet that real-world immersion plus AI-assisted self-study beats classroom instruction for technical/entrepreneurial skill-building.
Students meet weekly in pods of about eight with a mentor to report what they did the prior week and commit to what they'll do next, a structure explicitly modeled on Oxford's tutorial system, YC, a16z Speedrun, and Alpha School.
framework🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: mediumHorowitz Andreessen AcademyAlpha School
So what: A lightweight, low-cost accountability mechanism (peer pods + mentor check-ins) that could be replicated cheaply by other bootcamp/accelerator-style programs.
The founding class fellowship will accept only 50 students, with a stated ambition to scale to 500 or even 1,000 over time.
data_point🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: mediumHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: Small initial cohort size implies extreme selectivity and scarcity-driven brand-building before scaling, similar to early YC batches.
Admissions favor demonstrated 'slope' (rate of learning/improvement) over static credentials or age of first venture; the second-round interview process includes IQ testing, personality testing, and writing testing, following an initial portfolio submission.
framework🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: A concrete, unusual admissions methodology (formal IQ/personality/writing testing at a startup-backed school) that could draw regulatory or media scrutiny, worth watching for how it's received.
A 16-year-old student named Dar in Tennessee built an LLM for biology design that beat some leading models and has since pivoted to protein design after identifying it as the larger market opportunity.
anecdote🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: mediumHorowitz Andreessen Academy

AI-in-education stance: doomerism vs. optimism 7
Biyani explicitly expects most current 'AI doomer' students (citing recent graduation-ceremony booing of AI, e.g. at Columbia) to be a poor fit for the academy, and is deliberately recruiting AI optimists instead.
thesis🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: Signals a generational split among Gen Z toward AI that a16z is betting will resolve in favor of AI-embracing builders; positions the academy as a countercultural brand against campus AI backlash.
“I believe that most students who dislike AI are not going to be a fit for the academy.” ▶ 32:22
In contrast to New York City schools banning AI in classrooms, the academy will fully embrace AI and provide additional compute credits and funding so students can build with it, rather than restrict access.
contrarian🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: A direct positioning bet against the current K-12/public-school trend of restricting AI tools, testing whether an unrestricted-AI education model outperforms.
Even if one believes in an AI-driven jobs 'doomer' future, Biyani argues the correct response is to become part of the top 1% of AI-native builders, since that cohort will be the most in-demand regardless of how automation plays out.
framework🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen Academy
So what: A hedging framework useful beyond this school: skill differentiation into 'AI-native builder' status is presented as insurance against labor-market disruption from AI.
The curriculum explicitly trains students to orchestrate 30-50+ AI agents simultaneously (including agents orchestrating other agents), reflecting a belief that the highest-value skill in the coming labor market is agent-orchestration architecture.
prediction🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highHorowitz Andreessen AcademyMaven

“You should come in and learn how to orchestrate 30 agents at a time. 50 agents, I don't know, orchestrate agents that are orchestrating agents that orchestrating agents.” ▶ 34:30
Biyani sees a broader labor-market shift from middle managers who coordinate teams of humans toward 'super ICs' who personally direct large numbers of AI agents; his company Maven is built entirely around teaching this transition.
thesis🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: highMaven
So what: A structural labor-market thesis: middle-management roles are being disintermediated by AI-agent orchestration, a trend relevant to enterprise software and future-of-work investment theses.
“It used to be that it was really valuable to be a middle manager... And now we have the rise of the super IC.” ▶ 34:00
Biyani's personal rule (used at his own company, not mandated at the academy) is that a document's first draft must be written by the human before AI is used to refine and stress-test the thinking, to preserve genuine problem-solving skill.
anecdote🗣 Gagan Biyaniconviction: mediumMaven
So what: A concrete, transferable AI-usage policy for knowledge-work teams trying to avoid skill atrophy while still using AI tools heavily.

New Era Finance Podcast 1 episode(s) · 30 insights

Michael Howell: FED Rate Hikes Will Trigger The Biggest Bull Run Ever57 min30 insights2026-09-22
▶ Watch on YouTube
Michael Howell (Capital Wars) argues the West's debt overhang has turned financial markets into pure debt-refinancing engines, forcing governments to fund themselves at the short end (quasi money-printing) and keeping gold and Bitcoin as the key monetary-inflation hedges, even as equities face looming PE-multiple compression and 10-year yields push toward 6%.
Debt refinancing regime & no path to austerity · 3Monetary inflation as the master mechanism · 4The debt-liquidity cycle: framework and indicators · 3Bessent/Fed liquidity signaling and the rate-hike paradox · 5China, PBOC liquidity and the gold/crypto link · 3Equities and the AI bubble parallel · 4Bond market outlook and portfolio math · 5Systemic risks and falsifiability · 3
Debt refinancing regime & no path to austerity 3
Financial markets have structurally shifted from being new-capital-raising mechanisms to debt-refinancing engines, with roughly 80% of all primary transactions in global capital markets now being debt refinancing rather than funding new investment.
framework🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highglobal capital marketsdebt refinancing
So what: In a refinancing-dominated market, balance-sheet/liquidity capacity to roll over debt matters more than the cost of capital (interest rates) when forecasting asset prices.
“We would reckon that about 80% of all primary transactions in capital markets worldwide are now debt refinancing transactions.” ▶ 11:49
US public debt has grown roughly 12x since year 2000, and the federal government is running a persistent fiscal deficit of about 6% of GDP with no sign of narrowing.
data_point🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highUS public debtUS fiscal deficit
So what: A structurally compounding deficit reinforces the case for long-run currency debasement and hard-asset allocation over cash and long bonds.
“public public debt is probably something like uh what 12 times bigger than it was in uh uh in year 2000” ▶ 5:52
Aging demographics across the West combined with an escalating US-China 'capital war' over dollar-versus-yuan dominance make austerity politically and strategically impossible, and Howell warns that competition could eventually morph into military conflict, meaning governments will keep spending and financing deficits through money creation rather than tax hikes or cuts.
thesis🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highUnited StatesChinademographicsfiscal policy
So what: Removes austerity as a realistic policy path and flags great-power conflict as a tail risk, both of which argue for holding monetary-inflation and geopolitical hedges (gold, crypto) over long-duration bonds.
“those capital wars for example between the US and China uh for the dominance of um of um the dollar or the yuan system uh in the long term that is likely to morph into maybe military conflict at some stage” ▶ 1:43
Monetary inflation as the master mechanism 4
Governments, led by the US, are increasingly funding themselves with short-dated 3-6 month Treasury bills rather than long bonds; this now makes up about a quarter of total debt issuance and is set to grow toward a third, which Howell treats as a quasi money-printing mechanism because banks buy the bills and expand their balance sheets and the broader money supply.
framework🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highUS TreasuryTreasury billsmoney supply
So what: The rising share of short-bill issuance is a trackable leading indicator of accelerating monetary inflation, ahead of any change in headline policy rates.
“that percentage of debt issuance which is currently about a quarter of total debt issuance is now in in this short-term these short-term instruments it's going to grow to probably a third”
Howell argues the real story is 'monetary inflation' (currency debasement that erodes the wealth of anyone not holding inflation-hedge assets), not 'financial repression' as commonly framed by the media, and that main-street inflation and monetary inflation are distinct but related destructive forces.
framework🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highmonetary inflationfinancial repression
So what: Reframes the debt/inflation debate: the losers are savers in non-hedge assets, not just consumers facing CPI, which argues for actively rotating into gold/crypto rather than assuming standard inflation protection (e.g., TIPS) is sufficient.
“we've argued that, you know, it's slightly misguided to talk about financial repression. That's not really the the full story. The full story is you're getting monetary inflation.” ▶ 6:33
Over the past 10 years, Bitcoin has shown roughly a 10x multiplier to global liquidity changes -- a 10% increase in global liquidity has corresponded to roughly a doubling of Bitcoin's price -- meaning only a small portfolio allocation (a few percent) is needed for meaningful monetary-inflation-hedge protection.
data_point🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highBitcoinglobal liquidity
So what: Investors can treat a small (3-4%) Bitcoin allocation as an efficient, leveraged liquidity/inflation hedge rather than needing a large position to move portfolio outcomes.
“for every 10% increase in global liquidity, you get something like a a doubling of the Bitcoin price” ▶ 0:19
Howell's global liquidity sensitivity ranking places crypto (Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana) and precious metals (gold, silver) at the high-sensitivity end, while long-duration fixed income -- the yield curve and 10-year US Treasuries -- is negatively correlated to global liquidity, meaning bonds tend to fall in value precisely when liquidity is expanding.
framework🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highBitcoinEthereumSolanagoldsilver10-year Treasury
So what: Provides a ranked hedge list investors can use to reposition portfolios ahead of anticipated liquidity-regime shifts, overweighting crypto/precious metals and underweighting long bonds.
The debt-liquidity cycle: framework and indicators 3
Howell's 'debt-liquidity cycle' framework holds that debt requires liquidity to be refinanced while liquidity requires good-quality debt as collateral (roughly 80% of total liquidity creation is collateral-backed via repo); when the two become mismatched -- too much debt to refinance versus available liquidity -- the debt-liquidity ratio spikes, the system fails into a refinancing crisis, and central banks are forced to intervene because new credit structurally rests on old debt not being allowed to default.
framework🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highrepo marketcollateralcentral banks
So what: A rising debt-to-liquidity ratio is an early-warning signal for financial crisis and imminent central-bank money printing -- worth tracking as a systemic-risk gauge.
“debt needs liquidity for refinancing but equally liquidity needs good quality debt as collateral... central banks come in and support the system. They simply have to otherwise the system would fail.” ▶ 12:37
Howell says the key indicators for monitoring repo and collateral market stability are the SOFR-to-Fed-funds spread, the MOVE index (bond market volatility), and the term premium in bond markets, which is most easily proxied by watching the slope of the yield curve -- not headline policy interest rates.
framework🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highSOFRMOVE indexyield curveterm premium
So what: Gives investors a concrete, trackable dashboard for anticipating liquidity stress ahead of, and instead of, headline Fed rate decisions.
“the first indicator is to look at the repo markets uh and to look at what sofa spreads are. The second thing to look at is uh to look at uh bond volatility through the move index.” ▶ 55:23
Howell dismisses the popular 'four-year Bitcoin halving cycle' narrative, arguing the real driver of crypto cycles is a five-to-six-year debt-refinancing/liquidity cycle, implying traders waiting for a seasonal October bottom based on the four-year cycle model may be using the wrong framework and could be left behind.
contrarian🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highBitcoinliquidity cycle
So what: Investors timing crypto entries around halving-based four-year cycle models should instead track the debt-refinancing calendar and liquidity indicators per Howell's framework.
“there's there's no there's no four-year cycle as far as we can see. I mean the cycle is different. the cycle is more like a refinancing cycle based around debt which is a five to six year cycle.” ▶ 37:34
Bessent/Fed liquidity signaling and the rate-hike paradox 5
Howell says the market and media misread Treasury Secretary Bessent's move to double Treasury bond buybacks -- buybacks are too small to meaningfully influence yields -- arguing the real purpose is to suppress bond market volatility (the MOVE index) and keep the bond market orderly.
causal🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highBessentUS TreasuryMOVE indexbond buybacks
So what: Treasury buyback announcements should be read as volatility-suppression signals, not yield-management tools, when assessing their market impact.
“I think that what I mean I think the media in in many cases misread that Bessant move... What he's trying to influence is volatility in the market.” ▶ 16:52
Fed Chair Walsh's FOMC pledge to keep bank reserves at 'ample' levels is, per Howell, a signal aimed at keeping money markets and repo funding liquid and stable, working in tandem with Bessent's Treasury actions to jointly control collateral stability and repo liquidity.
causal🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highFed Chair Walshbank reservesrepo market
So what: Statements about bank reserve adequacy are a policy lever worth monitoring as closely as rate decisions, since they directly target the repo/collateral stability Howell says actually drives markets.
“one of the things that they intended to do was to keep uh bank reserves uh at uh adequate or ample levels” ▶ 17:44
Howell attributes Bitcoin's breakout to roughly $80,000 directly to the combined Bessent/Fed liquidity signals, viewing the rally as market confirmation that policymakers intend to keep funding the government at the short end and maintain ample system liquidity.
causal🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highBitcoinBessentgold
So what: Treasury/Fed liquidity-signaling events (buybacks, reserve-level guidance) can function as tradeable leading catalysts for crypto and gold rallies.
“my view is that crypto and gold took that as a cue and that's why they took off.” ▶ 20:31
Howell argues rate hikes in the current regime could be stimulative rather than contractionary: because the government is now a huge net borrower, a higher policy rate increases the interest income transferred to the private sector holding government debt, and he expects a 25bp hike could even cause long-end Treasury yields to rally as markets read it as reassurance the Fed is being disciplined -- the opposite of the mainstream media's expected reaction.
contrarian🗣 Michael Howellconviction: mediumFederal Reserveinterest rates10-year Treasury
So what: Investors shouldn't assume a hawkish Fed move is automatically bearish for risk assets or bonds in a high-debt regime; a hike could paradoxically ease bond-market stress.
“if you increase interest rates in the US, you're actually giving the private sector more cash. Well, that's a stimulus, not not a not a contraction.” ▶ 19:34
Fed Chair Walsh reviving M2/money-supply growth as an explicit part of the monetary policy framework, after decades of it being dropped, is per Howell a face-saving pivot that effectively buys the Fed one-to-three years before the monetary aggregates start signaling serious trouble.
contrarian🗣 Michael Howellconviction: mediumFed Chair WalshM2 money supply
So what: M2 growth reappearing in Fed communications is itself a signal worth tracking as an admission that liquidity, not just rates, is now central to policy -- and a marker for how much runway remains before trouble surfaces.
“it may well be uh one, two, three years before the monetary aggregates really start to spell trouble. And that means they're buying time.” ▶ 23:28
China, PBOC liquidity and the gold/crypto link 3
Howell argues the primary catalyst for gold's rally was not the Russia-Ukraine war but the People's Bank of China printing money to manage what he calls the largest debt burden of any major economy.
causal🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highPBOCChinagoldRussiaUkraine
So what: PBOC liquidity data may be a better leading indicator for gold price direction than geopolitical headlines, and is worth tracking directly rather than inferring gold moves from war news.
“contrary to what many commentators argue that the gold market, uh, catapulted higher after the Russian invasion of Ukraine, actually the key catalyst was the People's Bank of China printing money.” ▶ 31:17
Because China bans crypto trading, capital responding to Chinese monetary debasement flows mainly into gold, but Howell's Capital Wars research found PBOC liquidity still has a measurable, likely indirect effect on crypto prices, probably spilling over via the real economy and the gold market.
data_point🗣 Michael Howellconviction: mediumPBOCChinacryptogold
So what: PBOC liquidity is a global cross-asset variable worth including in crypto price models even though China formally excludes crypto from its financial system.
“the People's Bank of China has a big impact on the crypto markets too... it's having a big effect, too.” ▶ 32:50

“You haven't really had the great debasement in the west yet. That's coming. Okay, that's that's cream on top, right?” ▶ 32:13

Howell expects equity indices to underperform going forward: as bond yields rise and liquidity leaves the financial sector for the real economy, PE multiples come under downward pressure even as earnings still grow for now, and he projects weaker earnings growth alongside further multiple contraction materializing around 2027.

Howell admits his prior call that Wall Street would be rangebound this year was wrong, because real economic strength delivered a much bigger earnings surprise than he expected, which kept PE multiples elevated despite rising bond yields.
