Bond market outlook and portfolio math 5
Howell projects 10-year US Treasury yields will test 6% and possibly go higher, based on current nominal GDP growth of roughly 7-8% (a level last seen in the mid-1980s, when bond yields were around 8%) and the expectation that yields converge toward nominal growth rates.
prediction🗣 Michael Howellconviction: high10-year Treasurynominal GDPbond yields
So what: A specific, falsifiable level (6% on the 10-year) to watch for confirming or rejecting the higher-for-longer bond yield thesis.
“I would fully expect yields at the 10-year uh tenor in the US to test 6% uh in the you know in the foreseeable future um and possibly go higher.” ▶ 27:17
A COVID-era 'debt maturity wall' -- debt that was refinanced out to the later 2020s during the pandemic -- is now coming due in the next few years, adding a major additional refinancing load on top of ongoing deficit financing, which will require central banks to keep money-market liquidity ample.
data_point🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highdebt maturity wallcentral banksCOVID-19
So what: Gives a concrete forward catalyst and rough timeline for renewed liquidity injections -- worth tracking upcoming Treasury and corporate refinancing calendars for the late 2020s.
“there was a lot of debt then that was refinanced uh to the later years of this decade uh the so-called debt maturity wall which is coming back uh in the next few years” ▶ 48:17
Howell frames bond investing as a straightforward math exercise: at a 5% starting yield with roughly 10-year duration, a 100bp rise in yields produces about a 10% capital loss offset by the 5% carry for a net loss of roughly 5%, meaning the higher the starting yield base, the more attractive bonds become as a holding again.
framework🗣 Michael Howellconviction: mediumbond yieldsdurationfixed income
So what: A simple mental model for judging when higher-yield bonds re-enter 'buy' territory as an income/hedge asset after years of unattractive real returns.
“if you've got a 5% carry, that means you're only facing a 5% uh loss overall on your total return... the higher the yield base goes, the more attractive the bond markets are.” ▶ 49:42
Howell calls the COVID-era negative interest rate policy 'insanity,' noting that in four millennia of recorded interest-rate history (per Sydney Homer's 'A History of Interest Rates,' required reading at Salomon Brothers where Howell worked) there is no precedent for zero or negative rates, and that negative rates perversely incentivize taking on more debt -- the opposite of what the system needs.
contrarian🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highnegative interest ratesSydney HomerSalomon Brothers
So what: Frames the post-2020 return to positive real/nominal rates as a reversion to historical norms rather than an aberration, reinforcing the view that the negative-rate era was itself the anomaly.
“nowhere in that pages is there any reference at all to zero or even negative interest rates. It just didn't happen... it's a mad world because if you have negative interest rates you clearly incentivize people to take on more debt.” ▶ 50:44
Since year 2000, Howell notes the S&P 500 is up roughly 6-7x, gold is up roughly 12-13x, and Bitcoin's gain is a further order of magnitude beyond that -- evidence he cites for the monetary-inflation-hedge thesis over multi-decade horizons.
data_point🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highS&P 500goldBitcoin
So what: A long-horizon performance comparison supporting overweighting hard-asset/monetary-inflation hedges over equities as the primary store-of-wealth allocation.
“since year 2000 I think I'm correct in saying that the S&P is up six or seven times... but the gold market is up 12 13 times and bitcoin is some magnitude more than that” ▶ 51:47
Systemic risks and falsifiability 3
Howell's key tail risk to the debt-refinancing/liquidity system is opaque, overlapping balance sheets from the growth of shadow banking (A lends to B, B lends to C, C lends to D, and so on), where a single failure can cascade into a multitude of interconnected failures -- the scale of this overlap is unknown but suspected to be large.
contrarian🗣 Michael Howellconviction: mediumshadow bankingbalance sheet
So what: A hidden systemic-risk vector to monitor -- interconnected non-bank lending chains -- that standard leverage metrics don't fully capture and could amplify a localized shock.
“before you know it you get one failure and you get uh that gives rise to a multitude or or or 26 you know alphabetic failures and so the whole thing is multiplied and that that's really the risk.” ▶ 35:27
The Federal Reserve's Standing Repo Facility, designed to backstop money markets in a crisis, has never been genuinely stress-tested, and Howell expects it will likely fail in a real crisis (as such mechanisms typically do), forcing the Fed to fall back on printing more money.
prediction🗣 Michael Howellconviction: mediumStanding Repo FacilityFederal Reserve
So what: Investors shouldn't over-rely on the SRF as a guaranteed crisis backstop; its real test is still ahead, and its likely failure mode is more, not less, monetary expansion.
≈ “we hope that is a good uh crisis uh protection tool. We don't know because it hasn't really been tested and the reality is that it probably will fail in a crisis because these things always do.” paraphrase
Howell states the only scenario in which his monetary-debasement thesis would prove wrong is the discovery of an entirely new way to fund government debt without printing money -- something he says has no precedent in thousands of years of financial history -- and adds that even a worst-case kinetic war scenario would still ultimately be resolved through money printing to fund the conflict.
framework🗣 Michael Howellconviction: highgovernment debtmoney printing
So what: A clear falsifiability marker investors can use to test whether Howell's core thesis is being validated or broken over the coming years.
“in in thousands of years of history, I mean, that's that's never appeared... financial markets now debt refinancing mechanisms and in a world of debt refinancing it's basically all about money printing and that's that's that's it” ▶ 52:49

Why Reinsurance Could Become DeFi’s Best Collateral | Ryan Connor & Ayyan Rahman58 min29 insights2026-09-23

Henri's tokenized reinsurance product (onyc/ENYC) delivers double-digit, low-duration yield uncorrelated to crypto or equities, and has become Solana DeFi's most utilized RWA collateral (96% utilization, ~2/3 of supply looped on Kamino at 7%+ lending rates). Guests Ryan Connor (Rockaway X) and Ayyan Rahman (Henri co-founder) argue reinsurance's structural risk-return profile, improving climate/AI forecasting, and a still-tiny ~$300M onchain penetration of an $800B TAM make it a durable, underexploited yield source and DeFi collateral primitive.
Reinsurance Mechanics & Henri's Structure · 4Reinsurance Risk-Return vs Traditional Assets · 3Onchain Composability & Collateral Utility · 6Tail Risk & Liquidation Mechanics · 5Climate Data, AI & Hurricane Signal · 5TAM, Growth Trajectory & Broader RWA Thesis · 6
Reinsurance Mechanics & Henri's Structure 4
Reinsurance is insurance for insurance companies: primary insurers underwrite policies (e.g., Florida homeowner storm coverage) up to a threshold, then cede losses above that threshold to reinsurers in exchange for a premium.
framework🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: highreinsuranceinsurance
So what: Basic mechanism investors need to understand before evaluating any reinsurance-linked yield product.
Henri's ENYC (onyc) token is structured so that $1 deposited returns $1 of onyc, with 85 cents placed in a Bank of New York Mellon treasuries trust as underwriting collateral alongside the cedant's premium payments, and the resulting yield stream accrues to onyc holders.
data_point🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: highHenriENYConycBNY Mellon
So what: Clarifies exactly where onyc yield originates and how collateral is custodied, useful for underwriting the token's credit risk.
Henri underwrites primarily property catastrophe reinsurance across peak perils (US hurricanes, Japan typhoons, earthquakes, wildfires) plus a diversified book of worldwide catastrophe exposures, rather than concentrating in one geography like many traditional reinsurers.
data_point🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: highHenriproperty cat reinsurance
So what: Global diversification (versus a specialist reinsurer's regional book) is a risk-management differentiator worth checking against future disclosures.
Traditional reinsurance/ILS exposure has historically been gated to pension funds and private equity firms writing large checks, with multi-year lockups and only annual liquidity windows, and disclosure limited to quarterly reports on roughly a one-month lag.
data_point🗣 Ryan Connorconviction: highILS fundspension fundsprivate equity
So what: Explains the structural illiquidity/opacity gap that onchain reinsurance products like Henri are arbitraging.
≈ “I think the highest frequency data that you're going to get out of reinsurance funds in TRAFI is on a quarterly basis on a one-month lag” paraphrase
Reinsurance Risk-Return vs Traditional Assets 3
From 2006 onward, the S&P 500 annualized roughly 8% with about 15% volatility, while the Swiss Re Cat Bond Index annualized roughly 13% with about 2.8% volatility over the same period, giving reinsurance a markedly better risk-adjusted return profile than equities.
data_point🗣 Ryan Connorconviction: highS&P 500Swiss Re Cat Bond Index
So what: A concrete long-run risk/return comparison an allocator can cite when sizing a reinsurance sleeve versus equities.
“Reinsurance is annualizing I believe 13% keer uh the Swiss Recat Bond Index with 2.8% involved over the same period.” ▶ 12:25
Reinsurance functions as portfolio ballast for bond managers because it offers high yield with low duration and low correlation to the economic cycle, unlike stretching for yield into equity-like credit risk that becomes correlated to defaults and mark-to-market swings.
framework🗣 Ryan Connorconviction: highreinsurancebond portfoliosfixed income
So what: Frames reinsurance as a distinct diversifier bond managers can add without taking on correlated credit/equity risk.
Unlike private credit, where a default impairs an issuer's access to credit for the next four to five years, a major catastrophe loss event in property-cat reinsurance is typically followed by rates increasing significantly, making it the best time to write new reinsurance business.
causal🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: highproperty cat reinsuranceprivate credit
So what: Counterintuitive: loss events in reinsurance improve forward return prospects, unlike credit markets where defaults are persistently damaging — implies reinsurers should have dry powder to deploy post-event.
Onchain Composability & Collateral Utility 6
Roughly two-thirds of total onyc supply is deposited on Kamino (Solana lending market), making Henri's onyc the largest RWA market by deposits on that platform.
data_point🗣 hostconviction: highHenrionycKamino
So what: Concentration of a young RWA's float into one lending venue is a real usage signal but also a venue-concentration risk to monitor.
Lenders supplying USDC against onyc collateral on Kamino earn over 7%, versus the roughly 3-4% typical for DeFi lending, while being protected by an LTV structure that provides about a 28% first-loss buffer before their principal is at risk.
data_point🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: highKaminoonycUSDC

The Exponent protocol splits tokenized onyc into principal and yield components, letting participants speculate purely on the yield stream or separate exposure into senior and junior (credit-tranched) tokens for different risk appetites.

thesis🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: highRWAtokenization

Tail Risk & Liquidation Mechanics 5
Henri reserves 50% of total annual premiums as a buffer specifically because reinsurance risk is seasonal (e.g., concentrated in hurricane season), rather than releasing all premium income upfront, so that reserve layer absorbs losses before NAV/collateral is touched.
framework🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: highHenrireserving
So what: Explains the loss-absorption waterfall (earned premium -> reserved premium -> NAV/collateral) investors should model before sizing leverage.
“we reserve across the entire year 50% of total premiums” ▶ 28:32
Per Ali Labs' risk analysis, Henri's worst-case NAV drawdown scenario is modeled at roughly 20%, characterized as a one-in-100-year event.
data_point🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: highAli LabsHenriNAV
So what: Gives loopers/lenders a concrete tail-risk figure to stress-test their leverage and LTV assumptions against.
“the worst case scenario that we've done analysis for is like a 20% nav draw down scenario, which is like a one in 100year event” ▶ 24:09
In a prior severe Florida hurricane (around 2017-2019), at least one ILS fund lost its entire annual yield — investors received 0% instead of an expected ~11% — illustrating the realistic downside case for reinsurance exposure rather than a principal wipeout.
anecdote🗣 Ryan Connorconviction: mediumILS fundsFlorida hurricane
So what: Sets investor expectations: the typical tail outcome in this asset class is a lost year of yield, not necessarily large principal loss.
“there was like one ILS fund that lost all their yield for the year” ▶ 31:40
Because onyc's NAV is set by an offchain oracle rather than real-time market pricing, a catastrophe event does not trigger instant liquidations the way a live-priced asset would; Henri's underwriting team aims to update NAV onchain within about 24 hours of an event to keep the oracle price close to true value.
causal🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: mediumonycoracleNAVKamino
So what: The lag between real-world loss events and onchain NAV marks is a structural risk for leveraged loopers — a fast-moving event could leave collateral mispriced for up to a day.
Kamino and Ali Labs calibrate onyc's LTV and maximum leverage parameters against real historical catastrophe data (backtesting how past events would have impacted NAV) rather than generic risk assumptions, and Ali Labs publishes a dashboard letting users simulate liquidation thresholds at different leverage levels.
framework🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: highKaminoAli Labsonyc
So what: Actionable due-diligence step: loopers can use the Ali Labs dashboard to test their specific leverage against historical catastrophe scenarios before sizing a position.
Climate Data, AI & Hurricane Signal 5
NOAA data on landfall hurricanes shows a secular decline in frequency over the last three decades, and California wildfire frequency has been roughly constant — the apparent rise in catastrophe losses is driven mainly by increased building in fire- and storm-prone areas, not by more frequent events.
contrarian🗣 Ryan Connorconviction: highNOAAhurricanesCalifornia wildfires
So what: Challenges the popular narrative that climate change is straightforwardly increasing catastrophe frequency — reinsurance investors who get comfortable with this data can underwrite tail risk more confidently than the general public assumes.
“if you look at the data from Noah and you look at landfall hurricanes, you will see that they are in secular decline over the last three decades” ▶ 32:23
As of recording (peak 2025 Atlantic hurricane season), the ACE (Accumulated Cyclone Energy) index measuring storm intensity is at its lowest level in the 60-year historical dataset, implying an unusually low realized probability of a major landfall event this season so far.
data_point🗣 Ryan Connorconviction: mediumACE indexhurricane season
So what: A live, checkable data point (ACE index) that reinsurance-exposed investors can monitor in real time as a leading indicator for Henri's Q3/Q4 NAV risk.

AI weather models (referenced by the guests as a Google storm-prediction service) have become more accurate than some national weather administrations at forecasting hurricane paths and formation, enabling reinsurance participants to better hedge or take out puts on their book ahead of storms.
data_point🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: mediumAIGooglehurricane forecasting
So what: Improving AI forecast accuracy is a structural tailwind for reinsurer loss ratios and a potential edge source for actively-hedged books.
Hurricane forecasting has improved so much that today's 7-day forecast is roughly as accurate as a 1-day forecast was 20 years ago, representing a secular tailwind that should reduce loss volatility for the reinsurance asset class over time.
causal🗣 Ryan Connorconviction: highhurricane forecastingreinsurance

“the 7-day forecasts today are as accurate as like the one-day forecast like 20 years ago” ▶ 37:01

prediction🗣 Ayyan Rahmanconviction: mediumAIreinsurance brokers
So what: Implies AUM growth in reinsurance-linked products (including onyc) could accelerate as distribution costs fall, without necessarily eroding the yield investors capture.

NEAR Intents mechanics, TVL and risk model 7
NEAR Intents TVL was reported at $190M on DeFiLlama with ZEC alone contributing $71M, but Ilia says DeFiLlama's figure misses the newly launched confidential balances -- the true combined TVL (non-confidential + confidential) is over $206M.
data_point🗣 Iliaconviction: highNEAR IntentsZcashDeFiLlama
So what: Public TVL trackers may be materially understating NEAR Intents' actual scale because confidential-balance products aren't yet indexed -- worth re-checking DeFiLlama coverage before using it as the sole size metric.
“the TVL is now over 200 uh 206 million and then uh I yeah I mean I think Zcash across both of them is yes 70 million” ▶ 38:21
NEAR Intents works by users signing a desired trade ('intent') that is broadcast to competing solvers who bid quotes; the best quote settles through a 'verifier' smart contract and an MPC network that controls addresses on each external chain (Bitcoin, Solana, Zcash, etc.), replicating what centralized exchanges/custodians do but in a decentralized, programmable way.
framework🗣 Iliaconviction: highNEAR IntentsNEAR ProtocolBitcoinSolanaZcash
So what: Explains the actual settlement mechanism investors are trusting when they route swaps through NEAR Intents -- MPC custody plus a solver auction, not a simple bridge.
“we can effectively like what normally things centralized exchanges or even custodians do. They effectively put that on chain and we made it programmable” ▶ 36:25
NEAR's 'confidential' tech stack (MPC + secure enclaves + zero-knowledge proofs) is deliberately different from Zcash's user-generated ZK proofs: Zcash has no shared computable state (limiting it to payments), whereas NEAR's private-shard nodes can compute over shared state, enabling fully programmable private DeFi -- trading, lending/earn, and perpetuals -- not just transfers.
framework🗣 Iliaconviction: highNEAR ProtocolZcashNEAR Intents
So what: Clarifies the product differentiation thesis: NEAR is positioning as the programmable-DeFi complement to Zcash's pure store-of-value privacy, not a competitor to it.
“we're leveraging a combination of multi-party computation, secure enclaves and zero knowledge groups... we call it confidential because indeed it's different from kind of more userfacing um privacies that Z uh Zcash is offering” ▶ 28:03
Settlement can route funds directly into a Zcash shielded address, meaning a user can swap into or out of ZEC via NEAR Intents while the destination funds remain private end-to-end.
data_point🗣 Iliaconviction: highNEAR IntentsZcash
So what: A concrete product capability (shielded-address settlement) that differentiates NEAR Intents from typical cross-chain bridges for privacy-conscious users.
“it can release it directly into Zcash shielded address. Right? So I can give it a shielded address directly” ▶ 37:32
Ilia frames user risk on NEAR Intents as time-bounded: funds are only at risk of the MPC/bridge stack while a transaction is in flight between chains; once settled at the destination address, the user holds the asset natively with no ongoing counterparty exposure -- unlike opting into full confidential balances, which carries continuous exposure to the MPC/upgrade stack.
framework🗣 Iliaconviction: mediumNEAR IntentsNEAR Protocol
So what: A risk-assessment heuristic for anyone routing funds through NEAR Intents: transient bridging risk is lower than holding an ongoing confidential balance on the platform.
“you're actually only taking risk while like in any of this component of the system while your funds are moving right so effectively as soon as they arrive to the destination address right you effectively done” ▶ 49:00
NEAR runs an AI-driven monitoring/prevention system called 'Shield' that detects anomalous transactions and can pause a chain's activity; it triggered during a malicious Litecoin fork that exploited a privacy-pool double-spend, disconnecting that network from new NEAR Intents transactions until it stabilized, after only partial funds had moved through.
anecdote🗣 Iliaconviction: highNEAR IntentsLitecoinNEAR Protocol
So what: A live incident showing NEAR's cross-chain security layer functioning proactively rather than reactively -- a relevant data point for assessing counterparty/bridge risk across the ecosystems NEAR Intents supports.

Zcash's Orchard counterfeit-mint vulnerability and its resolution 3
A vulnerability discovered in Zcash's Orchard shielded pool in mid-2026 could have allowed an attacker to mint counterfeit ZEC undetected, because in a fully shielded pool with no shared visible state, there is no way for any observer -- including the network itself -- to detect that inflation occurred.
data_point🗣 hostconviction: highZcashOrchard pool
So what: Illustrates a structural risk unique to fully-anonymous protocols: undetectable supply inflation is possible precisely because privacy removes the ability to audit total state.
“the orchard pool itself, since it's a fundamental pool or was a fundamental pool in Zcash, you could print new coins in there” ▶ 40:26
Zcash fixed the Orchard counterfeit risk by migrating to a new 'Ironwood' pool with a turnstile mechanism enforcing that outputs cannot exceed inputs; over 90% of funds migrated (with forced migration triggered by any Orchard transaction), and the new design was formally verified three times to mathematically prove undetectable counterfeit bugs cannot exist.
data_point🗣 Mertconviction: highZcashOrchard poolIronwood
So what: Gives investors a concrete resolution timeline/mechanism to point to when assessing whether the Orchard vulnerability overhang on ZEC is actually closed.
“it was formally verified three times over with also a new proof that shows that undetectable counterfeit bugs mathematically cannot exist” ▶ 41:34
This class of vulnerability is specific to Zcash's fully-anonymous, no-shared-state architecture; other smart-contract platforms (including NEAR's private-shard model) retain internal monitoring/alerting nodes that can detect anomalies and retroactively patch issues, so a similar bug would not be fatal to those networks.
framework🗣 Mertconviction: mediumZcashNEAR Protocol
So what: A differentiating risk factor between 'maximal privacy' chains and 'confidential-but-monitored' chains -- relevant to comparative risk-weighting of ZEC vs. NEAR-based private assets.
“that's like a very specific problem to uh like an extremely anonymous protocol” ▶ 42:51

Formal verification and AI-driven security as the new DeFi security bar 2

data_point🗣 Iliaconviction: mediumNEAR Protocolformal verificationOpenAI

Most Crypto Tokens Were Never Meant to Enrich You | Scott Melker47 min25 insights2026-09-22

Token Economics: From Narrative to Earnings · 6Bitcoin Cycle Read and Price Targets · 5Stablecoin Economics and L1 Consolidation · 4Robin Hood Chain and the Tokenized-RWA UX Unlock · 4Crypto Politics: The Clarity Act and the Grift Overhang · 6
Token Economics: From Narrative to Earnings 6
During the 2020-2021 ICO/TGE boom, VCs could get an early tranche (roughly 15-20%) of a token immediately at the token generation event and cash out for a 100x within weeks, before the project had shipped any product, which structurally incentivized dumping on retail rather than building.
causal🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highVCstoken generation event (TGE)tokenomics
So what: Explains why early-stage token allocations without long vesting cliffs are a red flag for retail buyers; longer VC lockups/delayed vesting is now viewed as a necessary structural fix.
“they could probably if this thing launched with enough hype, they could 100x on the first 20% that they took out and then forget they ever owned it and it was an investment they made a month ago.” ▶ 7:57
In hindsight, roughly 99% of tokens issued in the last cycle did not need a token at all; the token existed purely as a fast fundraising mechanism disconnected from whether the underlying project needed one to function.
contrarian🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highICOstokenomics
So what: A useful filter for evaluating any new token launch: ask whether the product actually requires a token, or whether the token is just a capital-raising wrapper.
≈ “the reality, and I think this was the hardest realization, was that 99% of those didn't need a token, right?” paraphrase
The next crypto cycle will not repeat the 'throw a dart and any token goes up 100x' dynamic of prior cycles; going forward, token value will accrue specifically to projects with demonstrated utility and earnings rather than to assets simply because they are part of the crypto market or attached to a hot narrative.
framework🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highaltcoinstokenomics
So what: Investors should shift screening criteria from narrative/TAM stories toward cash-flow and buyback/earnings-accrual metrics when picking altcoin exposure.
“I don't think we'll be able to throw a dart and your token goes up 100x just just because it's part of the crypto market or is it assigned a narrative.” ▶ 8:27
Hyperliquid is cited as a rare example of a token an investor can value like a business: it runs large buybacks funded by real platform revenue and usage, giving it an earnings-based valuation case rather than a purely speculative one.
data_point🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumHyperliquid
So what: A concrete template for the 'value accrues to utility' thesis -- look for tokens with visible buyback mechanics tied to real revenue, not just TVL or narrative.
Zcash is one of the few smaller-cap assets that has genuinely 'captured' a durable narrative -- privacy -- and re-rated sharply as a result, distinguishing it from the broader pool of altcoins that are down over 90% from their peaks.
data_point🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumZcash
So what: Narrative capture (owning a clear, singular use-case category like privacy) is flagged as one of the few remaining paths for a smaller-cap token to outperform in this cycle.
“Zcash has captured the privacy narrative and is has gone kind of wild.” ▶ 5:52
XRP is the one asset Scott Melker says he would never touch again, framing it as the archetype of a project that raised billions of dollars and only later, after its founders were already billionaires, began building real utility -- the incentive order he considers backwards.
contrarian🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highXRP
So what: A direct, named negative view from a high-profile crypto commentator that can be weighed against consensus bullish XRP narratives (ETF flows, Ripple settlement optimism).
“One coin you would never touch again. God, do I have to say it? XRP.” ▶ 42:12
Bitcoin Cycle Read and Price Targets 5
Bitcoin market bottoms unfold in two sequential phases: first a 'price capitulation' where holders panic-sell on a crash, followed by a longer 'time-based capitulation' where price goes flat and boring for months and believers quietly give up, especially while other assets like AI stocks, gold and silver are rallying and pulling attention away.
framework🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highBitcoin
So what: A reusable mental model for timing accumulation: the boring, low-attention sideways grind after a crash -- not the crash itself -- is typically the real bottoming phase to buy into.
“you get the time based capitulation, which is you get there and then you stay really really boring for a very long time and the people who believe just start to give up” ▶ 13:38
The bounce from the low-$60,000s to roughly $82,000 within about a week, accompanied by the largest liquidation of short positions in Bitcoin's history and a ~24% weekly move, is treated as strong confirmation that a bottoming process (not necessarily a full bull resumption) has occurred, even though the broader bear market isn't confirmed over.
data_point🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumBitcoin
So what: A specific, dated technical/positioning signal (record short liquidation + 24% weekly move) that can be cross-checked against derivatives data as a bottom-confirmation marker.
“you can't ignore the largest liquidation of shorts in history and a 24% move in a week and pretend it means nothing.” ▶ 14:07
Scott Melker's price target for Bitcoin in the next cycle, if the traditional four-year cycle pattern still holds, is roughly $220,000-$250,000, meaningfully above the prior cycle's ~$126,000 all-time high.
prediction🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: lowBitcoin
So what: A concrete, named price target to track against outcomes, offered with the caveat that his prior cycle's similar prediction ($200k+ for 2021) also missed.
“I think into the 200s, 250” ▶ 42:36
The prior cycle's top of $126,000 was a historically weak cycle in relative terms: it did not even double the previous all-time high, whereas past Bitcoin cycles typically saw 3.5x to 10x moves above the prior cycle's peak, a pattern break Scott Melker found surprising.
data_point🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumBitcoin
So what: Evidence that Bitcoin's cycle amplitude is structurally compressing as the asset matures and institutionalizes, which argues against extrapolating historical multiple-based price targets forward unadjusted.
“I was very surprised that 126,000 was the top of that cycle. Didn't even 2x the previous highs and we always saw at least three and a half, 7 10x, you know, previous cycle highs.” ▶ 43:16
Scott Melker's own retail advice is to dollar-cost-average into Bitcoin and ignore short-term price action, treating any price in the $40,000-$80,000 range as a 'liquidation sale' relative to his multi-decade target; he personally kept buying manually all the way down through the $60,000s and would prefer a lower price to accumulate at than a higher one.
framework🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highBitcoin
So what: A behavioral anchor for long-horizon investors: reframe drawdowns as discount buying opportunities rather than danger signals, conditional on a genuine multi-year time horizon.
“I just say dollar cost average Bitcoin and forget about it.” ▶ 10:38
Stablecoin Economics and L1 Consolidation 4
Circle's revenue model is fragile because roughly 90%+ of its revenue comes from investing the dollar reserves backing USDC into short-term Treasuries and cash; if interest rates fall back toward 1% or a zero-rate environment, that revenue effectively disappears entirely, unlike Tether, which faces the same exposure but isn't publicly accountable to shareholders for it.
causal🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highCircleUSDCTether
So what: Circle (CRCL) earnings are effectively a leveraged bet on short-term rates staying elevated; a Fed cutting cycle toward ZIRP is a direct structural threat to its reported revenue, independent of USDC market-cap growth.
“let's say 90% plus of their revenue simply comes from custodying the dollars that back USDC, right?” ▶ 24:17
Circle's ARC blockchain is best understood as a strategic hedge against its own rate-cut revenue risk -- it needs a second, transaction/blockchain-based revenue stream to replace the interest income that will erode as rates normalize down over time.
thesis🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumCircleARC
So what: Frames ARC's success or failure as an existential business necessity for Circle, not just an optional growth initiative -- worth monitoring transaction volume/activity on ARC as a leading indicator for Circle's diversification.
Despite Coinbase recently renewing its revenue-sharing deal with Circle (in which Coinbase reportedly receives roughly half of USDC's reserve-interest revenue), Coinbase also just joined the Stripe-led 'Open USD' consortium (built on Stripe's Tempo blockchain), which is a direct competitor to USDC -- a live conflict-of-interest signal in the stablecoin space.
data_point🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumCoinbaseCircleUSDCStripeTempo
So what: Coinbase hedging against its own Circle partnership by backing a rival stablecoin consortium suggests it sees USDC's dominance as not guaranteed -- worth watching for share shifts in stablecoin market cap toward Tempo-based issuance.
“Coinbase also just joined the Open USD consortium, which is in direct competition to Circle.” ▶ 26:28
The 'L1 war is over' claim (that Solana and Ethereum have won among existing general-purpose Layer-1s) is directionally correct but overstated, because new purpose-built chains like ARC (stablecoins) and Tempo (payments) can still succeed without displacing Ethereum or Solana, simply by capturing entirely new growth in stablecoins and payments; meanwhile most existing L1s and L2s are expected to become 'zombie chains' that fade away amid a now-excessive supply of blockspace.
thesis🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumSolanaEthereumARCTempo
So what: Reframes the L1 investing question from 'which chain wins the general-purpose war' to 'which niche-purpose chain captures a specific new growth vertical' -- a different screening lens for new chain launches.
≈ “I think most layer ones become zombie chains and completely disappear. I think most layer 2s become zombie chains and completely disappear.” paraphrase
Robin Hood Chain and the Tokenized-RWA UX Unlock 4
Robin Hood Chain's real unlock is UX, not novelty: moving funds from a Robin Hood brokerage account into the Robin Hood Chain wallet is frictionless enough for a crypto-non-native user, and the platform packages a roughly 6-8% DeFi yield in a way that doesn't feel like taking on 'crypto risk' to its millions of non-crypto-native retail customers.
anecdote🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumRobin HoodRobin Hood ChainDeFi
So what: A mainstream brokerage successfully abstracting away DeFi's complexity for millions of non-native users is a bigger distribution risk/opportunity for incumbent crypto exchanges than any single token launch -- watch RH Chain wallet/DeFi deposit growth as an adoption proxy.
“getting money from Robin Hood to Robin Hood chain, from the exchange to the wallet is simple enough that my mom could figure it out.” ▶ 18:21
Scott Melker predicts Robin Hood Chain will rank as a top-five blockchain by activity within 12 months, rather than fading into a footnote like prior memecoin/hype cycles (DeFi Summer, NFTs, the Meta/metaverse boom).
prediction🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumRobin Hood Chain
So what: A specific, checkable 12-month forecast to revisit against on-chain activity/TVL league tables for Robin Hood Chain.
≈ “Top five chain.” paraphrase ▶ 42:07
Crypto portfolio construction is increasingly a 'barbell': serious institutional-grade assets (Bitcoin, and to a lesser degree Ethereum/Solana/Hyperliquid) on one side, pure speculation on the other, with a thin, largely ignored middle -- roughly tokens ranked 10 through 1,000 on CoinMarketCap -- that attracts little investor interest.
framework🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highBitcoinEthereumSolanaHyperliquid
So what: A practical portfolio-construction lens: avoid the mid-cap 'dead middle' of the market and concentrate either in top-tier institutional assets or in explicitly speculative, high-conviction plays.
“Everything else in the middle is this skinny bar that nobody's interested in. Coins, you know, 10 through 1,000 on Coin Market Cap.” ▶ 19:42
Trading volume and attention in crypto are highly nomadic -- the same pool of 'degen' traders rotates between whichever chain currently has hype (Solana, Base, Robin Hood Chain, soon ARC), so a chain's current dominance is not sticky; Base may be losing share right now, but Coinbase's crypto-native position means it shouldn't be counted out long-term.
thesis🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumSolanaBaseCoinbaseRobin Hood ChainARC
So what: Chain-level 'market share' narratives (e.g. 'Base stole the meme trade') should be treated as short-lived rotations rather than durable competitive shifts when sizing positions tied to a specific chain's momentum.
“I think Bass might be struggling right now, but I wouldn't bet against Coinbase, right?” ▶ 21:06
Crypto Politics: The Clarity Act and the Grift Overhang 6
The DTCC has discussed targeting the settlement of quadrillions of dollars in tokenized assets by 2027, illustrating how far institutional blockchain-settlement infrastructure plans have advanced even though the market price of crypto assets has not yet reflected that adoption.
data_point🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumDTCC
So what: A concrete institutional-adoption data point and timeline (2027) worth tracking as a catalyst, even though the speaker argues this specific type of adoption is not itself directly investable.
The most investable version of crypto's promise is not Wall Street adopting blockchain 'plumbing' (e.g., DTCC settling on-chain, or JP Morgan settling with Wells Fargo via tokenized deposits) -- that's just infrastructure replacement with no retail investment angle -- but financial inclusion: someone in a country with a hyperinflating currency gaining access, for the first time, to tokenized US equities and stablecoin savings alongside Bitcoin, all in one wallet.
thesis🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highDTCCstablecoinsBitcointokenized stocks
So what: Reframes where the real addressable-market growth in crypto sits: emerging-market retail access to tokenized US assets and stablecoin savings, not incremental efficiency gains inside existing Wall Street plumbing.
“someone in a foreign country who has never had access to United States capital markets, who could never participate in the stock market, and can now go on any exchange they want and buy a real tokenized version of a share, and they can buy it with a stable coin that's not in their hyperinflating currency.” ▶ 39:01
The Senate vote framed in the media as 'the Clarity Act vote' was actually only a procedural motion to allow debate on the bill, not a vote on final passage; conflating the two overstates how close the Clarity Act actually is to becoming law.
data_point🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highClarity Act
So what: A factual correction that matters for anyone trading prediction markets or crypto-policy-sensitive assets around Clarity Act headlines -- procedural votes and final-passage votes should not be conflated.
“today is just a procedural vote. It's a motion. It's it's effectively a vote to decide whether it's worth debating and voting on the Clarity Act.” ▶ 30:15
Even a Democratic senator personally opposed to the Clarity Act may still vote yes on the procedural motion, because the crypto lobby (e.g., Fairshake) is powerful enough that voting no risks millions of dollars in opposition spending against them in the midterms, and voting yes today keeps negotiations open without locking in a final position.
causal🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: mediumClarity ActFairshake
So what: Highlights crypto Super PAC spending power as a real swing variable in legislative outcomes -- a factor to weight alongside stated party positions when handicapping crypto legislation.
“I think any Democratic senator will think twice about voting no if they think that it's going to cause the political lobby and fair shake to spend millions of dollars against them in the midterms.” ▶ 31:44
Even if the Clarity Act ultimately fails, pro-crypto regulators (SEC Chair Atkins and the CFTC's Celig) have already shown they will unilaterally reverse prior hostile rules -- such as SAB 121, which had barred large institutions from treating custodied crypto as an asset rather than a liability -- creating a favorable regulatory environment for roughly the next two to two and a half years, though such regulatory gains are more easily reversed by a future administration than legislation would be. Prediction markets were putting only about a 6% probability on the Clarity Act becoming law before the midterms.
thesis🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highSECCFTCClarity ActSAB 121
So what: Suggests crypto businesses should plan around a ~2-2.5 year favorable-but-reversible regulatory window via agency rulemaking rather than waiting on legislation, and treat Clarity Act 'passage' headlines skeptically given the ~6% market-implied odds.
“I think prediction markets are putting that like 6% by the way to actually become law.” ▶ 42:00
The Clarity Act is politically stuck largely because Democrats will not hand Trump a legislative win that could be read as legitimizing his and his family's crypto-related enrichment; the Trump/Melania memecoin launched three days before his inauguration (timed to avoid emoluments-clause restrictions that applied once he took office) and Hunter Biden's 'Laptop' memecoin -- which briefly reached a roughly $140 billion valuation on only about $50,000 of actual liquidity before collapsing about 99% -- are cited as the concrete grift examples poisoning the negotiations, particularly the unresolved ethics clause.
causal🗣 Scott Melkerconviction: highClarity ActDonald TrumpHunter Biden
So what: Political-family memecoin grifts are directly and causally linked to stalled crypto market-structure legislation -- a concrete mechanism explaining regulatory overhang beyond generic 'partisan gridlock,' and a reason to expect the ethics clause to remain the key blocking issue.
“Hunter Biden launched a token last week called Laptop given it was like kind of a paper pump, but it went to $140 billion.” ▶ 33:27

Strategy CEO Phong Le Explains the STRC Recovery & Strategy's Future in Bitcoin50 min25 insights2026-09-22
▶ Watch on YouTube
Strategy CEO Phong Le explains how unexpected leverage (retail borrowing against Bitcoin at 6% to buy 12%-yield STRC) drove STRC's crash from par to $75, details the USD-reserve-and-buyback mechanics used to claw it back to ~99, and lays out a framework for holding periods across Bitcoin, MSTR and STRC while positioning Strategy as the 'JP Morgan of Bitcoin' inside a projected $300 trillion digital credit opportunity.
Bitcoin-AI convergence thesis · 4STRC dislocation and recovery mechanics · 6Digital credit ecosystem, competition and consolidation · 5MSTR/STRC holding-period framework and capital structure · 6'JP Morgan of Bitcoin' positioning and TradFi/DeFi convergence · 4
Bitcoin-AI convergence thesis 4
Strategy's CEO frames Bitcoin and AI as the two most important technologies of the 21st century, arguing AI should evolve toward being decentralized, non-sovereign and commodity-like the way Bitcoin already is, rather than staying concentrated in a handful of companies' GPU clusters.
thesis🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highBitcoinAIStrategyOpenAI
So what: Watch for decentralized-compute/open-source AI models as a structural trend rather than assuming continued concentration among mega-cap AI infrastructure owners.
“AI could use a Satoshi Nakamoto right about now.” ▶ 2:00
He sizes the global public capital markets at roughly $150 trillion, with financial services (JPMorgan, Bank of America, Wells Fargo, Citibank, Morgan Stanley, etc.) making up about $25 trillion as the second-largest industry, and technology (the Mag 7 and SaaS) as the largest at roughly $45 trillion.
data_point🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highJPMorganBank of AmericaWells FargoCitibankMorgan StanleyMag 7
So what: Gives a concrete TAM framework for sizing how large a disruption Bitcoin (financial services) and AI (tech/SaaS) could represent if his framing is directionally right.
He predicts AI's next disruption wave after 'virtual AI' (software/SaaS) will be 'physical AI' — humanoid robots, self-driving cars and self-repairing machines — hitting global manufacturing, retail, oil/gas, consumer packaged goods and textiles.
prediction🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumAImanufacturingretailrobotics
So what: Flags physical AI / robotics-adjacent manufacturing and industrial names as a second, less-priced-in leg of the AI disruption trade beyond software.
He does not believe AI needs government regulation to become more open and decentralized, expecting market forces alone (citing Jensen Huang and Elon Musk's public comments favoring open-source AI) to push the frontier that direction, just as Bitcoin needed no regulatory mandate to survive.
contrarian🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumAIJensen HuangElon MuskBitcoin
So what: A bet against the thesis that AI regulation is necessary/imminent for open-source AI to gain share — relevant to how investors weigh regulatory risk in AI-model competition.
STRC dislocation and recovery mechanics 6
STRC (Strategy's perpetual preferred 'digital credit' instrument) fell from its $100 par value to about $75 between late May and June, driven not by fundamentals but by leverage: retail investors were borrowing against Bitcoin at roughly 6% to buy STRC's ~12% yield and pocketing the spread, so a Bitcoin price drop forced forced selling of STRC.
causal🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highSTRCStrategyBitcoinMSTR
So what: High-yield, low-volatility instruments attract embedded leverage that can amplify drawdowns unrelated to the issuer's own solvency — a pattern to watch for in other yield-bearing crypto-adjacent products.
“We did not expect the amount of leverage that came into the system.” ▶ 13:31
A large Strategy shareholder reportedly calls this dynamic the 'Minsky effect': the more an instrument achieves a high yield with low realized volatility, the more it invites leverage into the system, setting up a self-reinforcing fragility.
framework🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO), attributing to an unnamed large shareholderconviction: mediumSTRCMinsky effect
So what: A reusable mental model for spotting when a 'safe, high-yield' instrument is quietly becoming a leverage magnet — applicable beyond STRC to any low-vol/high-yield product.
“one of our large shareholders... calls this the Minsky effect.” ▶ 12:31
Strategy's fix was not raising the STRC dividend (which moved from 11% to 12% in steps without materially affecting price) but instead building a US dollar reserve now covering roughly 3 years of dividends (about $5.1 billion), authorizing buybacks of STRC and MSTR, and being willing to sell Bitcoin to fund dividends or buybacks.
data_point🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highSTRCMSTRStrategyBitcoin
So what: Signals that for yield-bearing crypto-treasury instruments, cash coverage and buyback credibility are the price-support levers that matter, not the headline coupon — a proxy investors can track for similar products.
“What was driving the price of STRC was primarily the size of the US dollar reserve and now US dollar cash and the coverage of the dividend we had.” ▶ 10:35
Strategy maintains two separate dollar pools: a restricted USD reserve (~$5.1B) that by board agreement can only be used to pay interest and dividends on preferreds/converts (not to buy back converts), and a more flexible USD cash pool (~$6.4-6.7B) that can be used to buy back STRC, MSTR or convertible notes.
data_point🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highSTRCStrategyconvertible notes
So what: Investors modeling Strategy's ability to defend STRC/MSTR prices should track these two pools separately since they have different legal uses and different implied support for different securities.
“The USD reserve is there to pay interest and dividends on our uh debt and on our preferreds.” ▶ 21:04
STRC's institutional ownership rose from about 20% to 30% of the base as the price fell into the $75-90 range and recovered, with institutions adding new capital (not merely retail reducing holdings), which the CEO reads as validation that the instrument has now 'been through a crisis' and is de-risked in institutional eyes.
data_point🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumSTRCStrategy
So what: A track record of surviving a drawdown-and-recovery cycle is being used as a credit-worthiness signal to attract long-duration institutional holders — worth monitoring STRC's institutional ownership % as a sentiment gauge.
“70% retail 30% institutional... Institutions increased their holdings and increased in size.” ▶ 15:57
Part of why STRC has stalled just under par (98-99 rather than 100) is that some buyers who entered at $75-90 were principal-appreciation traders, not yield seekers, and are selling out once the price nears par, creating a persistent supply overhang near the top.
causal🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumSTRC
So what: Explains a technical reason STRC may lag full par recovery even as fundamentals improve — a distinct trading dynamic from credit risk that traders in the name should factor in.
“They were in it to go from 75 to 99. They weren't in it for it to sit at 100 and get 12%.” ▶ 17:58
Digital credit ecosystem, competition and consolidation 5
Strategy frames the addressable market for 'digital credit' products like STRC as the traditional credit industry (private credit, private equity, corporate bonds, treasuries) worth roughly $300 trillion, against which Strategy's current issuance is only about $15 billion — implying very early-stage penetration.
thesis🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highSTRCStrategyprivate creditBlue Owl
So what: If credible, this framing implies enormous multi-year TAM headroom for Bitcoin-collateralized credit products, supporting a long-duration bull case on the sector rather than just Strategy's stock.
≈ “We thought we were going to compete with a $300 trillion credit industry.” paraphrase
A competing product called SATA, built by a team including 'M Cole,' offers a 13% daily dividend yield and is described by Le as an 'incredible innovation,' though it has smaller collateral coverage, liquidity and scale than Strategy's STRC.
data_point🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumSATASTRCStrategy
So what: A named competing digital-credit product to track for relative yield/coverage/liquidity comparisons against STRC as the sector matures.
“they've created now SATA which is 13%... it's a daily dividend which I find to be an incredible innovation.” ▶ 21:46
An entire third-party ecosystem has emerged building on top of Strategy's STRC without Strategy's involvement: yield-bearing stablecoins (cited builders 'Apex and Saturn'), leveraged 'digital yield' products offering roughly 25% yields, and tokenization platforms (cited Kraken with StockX, and 'Ono's'), plus an ETF-like product called Orange BTC launched out of Brazil.
data_point🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumSTRCKrakenStockXOrange BTCStrategy
So what: STRC is becoming a base-layer 'platform' asset that other fintech products are levering and tokenizing — a second-order way to gain exposure to the theme beyond MSTR/STRC directly, but also a source of hidden leverage risk in the wider ecosystem.
“We did not think we would become the platform.” ▶ 23:16
Le explicitly flags a risk in this build-on-top ecosystem: some of the DeFi protocols layering leverage on top of STRC-based yield products could unwind in stress, similar to the leverage dynamic that hit STRC itself.
contrarian🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: lowSTRCDeFi
So what: A candid acknowledgment from the issuer itself that leverage risk in Bitcoin-collateralized credit products extends beyond Strategy's own balance sheet into an unmonitored third-party layer.
“some of these DeFi protocols... also have leverage and... they could or could not unwind.” ▶ 24:55
Le expects the roughly 25 US Bitcoin treasury companies operating today to consolidate down to only two or three long-term winners, comparing the shakeout to Bitcoin mining companies that pivoted into AI/high-performance-compute data centers as that market matured.
prediction🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumBitcoin treasury companiesMetaPlanetSmarterWebBitMineStrategy
So what: Implies a coming wave of failures/pivots/M&A among smaller Bitcoin treasury vehicles; investors should differentiate survivors (balance sheet scale, liquidity, institutional trust) from the pack rather than treat the category as homogeneous.
“in the Bitcoin treasury space we have 25 companies in the US and... two or three will survive and win.” ▶ 40:17
MSTR/STRC holding-period framework and capital structure 6
Le lays out an explicit holding-period framework by instrument: hold Bitcoin itself for 5-10 years, hold MSTR common stock for 3+ years, hold STRC (digital credit) for roughly 6 months to 3 years, and hold plain US dollars if your horizon is under 6 months.
framework🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highBitcoinMSTRSTRC
So what: A concrete allocation rule investors can apply directly: match instrument choice (BTC/MSTR/STRC/cash) to actual holding-period tolerance rather than chasing yield or beta mismatched to time horizon.
≈ “if your holding period for MSTR is less than 3 to four years, you are going to see volatility.” paraphrase
MSTR's investment case assumes Bitcoin compounds at roughly 30% per year on average over a 10-year holding period (not every single year), and that MSTR is designed to be 'amplified Bitcoin' that outperforms in Bitcoin bull markets but underperforms/lags during flat or down periods like the year from October to the interview date.
thesis🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highMSTRBitcoinStrategy
So what: Sets an explicit base-rate assumption (30%/yr BTC CAGR) underlying MSTR's premium/leverage thesis that investors can stress-test or disagree with directly.
“you have to start with the belief that Bitcoin is going to go up 30% a year.” ▶ 36:05
Strategy's other preferreds — STRF (senior, cumulative, with investor/governance protections), STRK (convertible), STRD (junior, highest effective yield), and Stream (a European instrument) — have not yet traded through a full Bitcoin bull cycle and are, in Le's view, currently underpriced relative to how they should perform if Bitcoin rises toward $125,000-$200,000.
prediction🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumSTRFSTRKSTRDStreamBitcoin
So what: Flags STRF/STRK/STRD/Stream as relatively untested, potentially mispriced instruments worth watching specifically during the next Bitcoin bull leg toward the $125K-$200K range Le references.
≈ “when we see Bitcoin rise to 125 to 150 to 200... it'll be really interesting to see how those perform.” paraphrase
Le expects to eventually launch a 'STRU' ticker to round out the letter series of preferreds but does not anticipate many additional new products or new-country expansions beyond that, prioritizing instead getting STRC to par and growing it 'judiciously' as the platform layer for third-party innovation.
prediction🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: lowSTRCSTRUStrategy
So what: Suggests Strategy's near-term roadmap is consolidation/stabilization of existing instruments rather than rapid new-product proliferation — relevant for investors expecting a steady cadence of new preferred launches.
≈ “I don't think we need a STRU out there to finish... all the letters.” paraphrase
On executive compensation, Le argues pay should be tied to metrics that reflect per-share value accretion (e.g., growth in Bitcoin held per share) rather than raw equity/option grants tied only to share count, implicitly critiquing dilution-linked compensation structures like the one MetaPlanet faced backlash over before cutting its options pool.
framework🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumMetaPlanetSimon GerovichStrategy
So what: A governance lens investors can apply across Bitcoin treasury companies: check whether management comp is tied to per-share/BTC-per-share accretion versus raw share-count grants that dilute holders.
≈ “if adding Bitcoin per share means that the equity will outperform Bitcoin, that's a good thing... paying compensation... aligned with the number of shares, dilution, that wouldn't necessarily be a good thing.” paraphrase ▶ 38:26
95% of Phong Le's compensation, and that of Strategy's broader executive team, is tied to common stock performance, which he cites as evidence management incentives are aligned with shareholders during the drawdown.
data_point🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highMSTRStrategy
So what: A concrete, checkable governance data point for investors assessing management alignment at Strategy versus peers.
≈ “95% of my compensation is through the performance of the stock and that's true with our executive team.” paraphrase
'JP Morgan of Bitcoin' positioning and TradFi/DeFi convergence 4
Le explicitly rejects the idea that Bitcoin will 'disintermediate' banks or the US dollar, positioning Strategy's ambition to be the 'JP Morgan of Bitcoin' as building products, distribution and trust inside the traditional capital system rather than replacing it — a notably non-maximalist stance from a major Bitcoin treasury company CEO.
contrarian🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highBitcoinJPMorganStrategyJamie Dimon
So what: Signals Strategy's strategic self-positioning as a TradFi-integrated infrastructure player rather than a disruptor-outsider, which affects how investors should value it relative to pure-play 'anti-bank' Bitcoin narratives.
“I don't think Bitcoin is going to necessarily disintermediate the banks.” ▶ 46:41
He predicts that within about five years, the current distinction between 'decentralized finance' and 'traditional finance' will disappear entirely, just as 'e-commerce' merged into plain 'commerce' after the late 1990s, because TradFi firms are adopting digital products and digital-native firms are integrating with TradFi.
prediction🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumBitcoinStrategyDeFiTradFi
So what: A timing marker (roughly 2031) investors can use to judge how fast institutional Bitcoin-finance convergence plays out, and a reason to expect large banks to keep launching digital-asset products rather than resist them.
“In 5 years from now, we'll just talk about finance.” ▶ 27:47
Strategy holds roughly 4% of all Bitcoin in existence (about 845,000 BTC) on its balance sheet, valued at roughly $45-50 billion at points referenced in the conversation, against roughly $8 billion of convertible debt notional outstanding.
data_point🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: highStrategyBitcoinMSTR
So what: Core balance-sheet figures for modeling Strategy's asset coverage and solvency buffer against its preferred/debt stack.
“we have 4% of all the Bitcoin in the world.” ▶ 44:34
For Bitcoiners, using a Roth IRA to hold Bitcoin is framed as a way to avoid a taxable event on eventual liquidation — the example given is turning an initial $5,000 into as much as $500,000 and withdrawing it entirely tax-free.
data_point🗣 Phong Le (Strategy CEO)conviction: mediumBitcoinRoth IRA
So what: A concrete, actionable tax-optimization tactic for individual Bitcoin holders (or investors advising them) to consider versus taxable brokerage or self-custody accounts.
“If you do it in a Roth, you could turn $5,000 into a half million and you're going to take it all out entirely tax-free.” ▶ 26:52

President Alexander Stubb: Who Decides the New World Order? | Podcast | In Good Company50 min25 insights2026-09-23
▶ Watch on YouTube
Finnish President Alexander Stubb argues the post-1989 world order is dissolving into a contest between a reformed multilateral UN system and multipolar transactional chaos, with the Global South (not the East) gaining decisive weight, AI/compute/energy access becoming a new axis of power that already rivals nation-states, and Europe forced into a more geopolitical, defense-heavy posture by Russia and an unpredictable Trump administration.
Global South Ascendance & World Order Reset · 4AI Power, Big Tech vs. States, and the Governance Gap · 5European Defense Rearmament & NATO's Burden Shift · 6Ukraine War Endgame Mechanics · 3Europe's Geopolitical Awakening & the Trump Effect · 5Polarization and Social Cohesion as Political Risk · 2
Global South Ascendance & World Order Reset 4
The post-Cold War world order is undergoing its fourth major reset (after 1918, 1945, 1989), and the outcome will be decided by the Global South's choice between a reformed multilateral/UN-based order and a multipolar, transactional, conflict-prone system.
thesis🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highUnited NationsGlobal Southmultilateralism
So what: Investors should track UN Security Council reform and Global South coalition-building (not just US-China rivalry) as the key variable for how stable or fragmented the geopolitical/trade environment becomes over the next decade.
“The most important message is that the world order is changing much like it changed in 1918, 1945 and 1989.” ▶ 1:14
Western dominance is ending not because the East (China) is taking over, but because the Global South (India, Gulf states, Nigeria, South Africa, Egypt, Brazil, Argentina) now has the population, economic, and technological scale to set terms itself.
thesis🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highIndiaGulf StatesNigeriaSouth AfricaEgyptBrazilArgentinaChina
So what: Reframes the standard 'US vs China' investment narrative; capital flows, demand growth and diplomatic leverage should increasingly be modeled around a fragmented multipolar Global South rather than a simple bipolar rivalry.
China has consistently outplayed Europe in the Global South by funding hard infrastructure (airports, highways, hotels), while European leaders show up mainly to lecture on governance, entrenching a durable Chinese soft-power and commercial advantage across Africa and other developing regions.
causal🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highChinaAfricaBelt and RoadEuropean Union
So what: Chinese-linked infrastructure, construction and commodities plays in Africa/Global South carry an entrenched incumbency advantage over European competitors unless European policy shifts from criticism to co-investment.
≈ “A western leader came to an African country, landed at an airport which was built by the Chinese, drove down a highway which was built by the Chinese... saying that you should not be cooperating with the Chinese.” paraphrase
Stubb and Mark Carney argue a fourth power bloc is emerging built on compute, data and AI, meaning technology leadership itself is becoming a geopolitical power center alongside (not just within) the US, China and the Global South, and access to it should be structured as win-win rather than zero-sum.
framework🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: mediumMark Carneyartificial intelligencecompute
So what: Frames AI infrastructure (compute capacity, data, chips, energy) as a distinct strategic asset class investors and policymakers should track the way they track military or trade power, not merely as a tech-sector story.
AI Power, Big Tech vs. States, and the Governance Gap 5
Stubb explicitly questions whether Anthropic or Facebook (Meta) is now more powerful than sovereign states like Norway or Finland, and says political science has no clear answer to the question.
contrarian🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: mediumAnthropicFacebookNorwayFinlandBig Tech
So what: Signals that even sitting heads of state now treat frontier AI labs and platform companies as peer-level power centers to nation-states, which should raise the bar for how investors price regulatory/geopolitical risk around Big Tech and AI labs.
≈ “So you can ask the question, you know, is Anthropic or is Facebook more powerful than Norway and Finland? And I think it's a legitimate question and political science can't necessarily give you the answer.” paraphrase
Dominance in AI requires two specific inputs: compute/data capacity and energy (electricity), and global power balance between the US, China, Europe and the Global South will hinge on differential access to these two resources.
framework🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highartificial intelligencecomputeelectricityenergy
So what: Reinforces the thesis that power grid capacity, chip supply and data-center energy access are now strategic bottlenecks worth tracking as directly as semiconductor export controls.
≈ “If you want to dominate you need two things: you need compute... and secondly you need energy or more specifically electricity.” paraphrase ▶ 5:20
Democracies are structurally losing the adaptation race to social media and AI because democratic institutions were designed in the 1700s to be deliberately slow and consensus-based, unlike the always-on reaction speed modern technology demands.
contrarian🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highdemocracysocial mediaartificial intelligence
So what: Supports a base case of continued regulatory lag and policy whiplash around AI/platforms in democracies, favoring jurisdictions or firms that can move faster than legislatures (both a risk and an opportunity for AI-first companies).
≈ “Democracies have not been able to cope with social media and with modern technology yet, and they're losing the struggle.” paraphrase
AI could either equalize opportunity globally or amplify injustice depending on near-term policy choices, and the mix of biotechnology with AI (DNA manipulation, genome sequencing) risks creating a Harari-style 'useless class' by removing the economic leverage labor historically held even under capitalism.
thesis🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: mediumartificial intelligencebiotechnologyYuval Hararilabor markets
So what: A structural labor-displacement risk that could reshape political stability and consumption patterns over the next decade; worth watching as a driver of populist/polarization risk premiums.
Because formal regulation lags fast-moving technology by one to three years, Stubb argues governance should rely on dedicated frontier-AI control bodies (citing Demis Hassabis's AI-safety work) starting in the US, then Europe and China, before eventually rolling up into a UN-level governance structure.
framework🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: mediumDemis HassabisUnited NationsAI regulationUnited StatesChinaBill Gates
So what: Suggests the realistic near-term AI governance path is technical/lab-led safety bodies rather than binding international law, which matters for how investors assess frontier-AI regulatory tail risk (e.g., catastrophic cyberattack scenarios) versus compliance-cost risk.
≈ “We need some kind of control bodies that can deal with this and make sure that frontier AI doesn't give us an apocalyptic result in for instance cyber attacks.” paraphrase
European Defense Rearmament & NATO's Burden Shift 6
NATO members are raising the defense-spending target from 2% to 5% of GDP, with defense industries described as 'booming' across both Europe and the United States.
data_point🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highNATOdefense spendingEuropeUnited States
So what: Directly supports a multi-year European/US defense-industrial capex and order-book tailwind; a concrete spending-target data point to size the sector opportunity.
≈ “I wouldn't say an alliance is in a crisis when it's increasing its defense expenditure from 2% to 5%, when defense industries are booming all around Europe and the United States.” paraphrase
Finland maintained high defense spending and preparedness after the Cold War (despite not joining NATO until 2023) due to memory of the 1939-1944 wars with the Soviet Union, buying 64 F-18 fighter jets, keeping male conscription, and buying secondary-market artillery.
anecdote🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highFinlandRussiaNATOF-18 fighter jets
So what: A concrete case study of sustained defense-industrial demand driven by historical threat memory rather than current-year headlines — a template for assessing which countries will sustain elevated defense capex even after any Ukraine ceasefire.
Because Finland's defense posture was already highly NATO-compatible, its 2023 NATO accession negotiations were the fastest in the alliance's history after applying following Russia's 2022 invasion of Ukraine.
causal🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highFinlandNATORussiaUkraine
So what: Illustrates how pre-existing interoperability (equipment standards, doctrine) compresses political/accession timelines — relevant for gauging how fast other partner states (e.g., further NATO enlargement candidates) could formalize alliances.
NATO is shifting from a US-anchored posture toward one where European members take more responsibility for conventional forces (land, air, navy, cyber, space) as the US signals it will not provide certain capabilities even in peacetime or crisis, requiring Europe to build domestic replacements.
prediction🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highNATOEuropeUnited States
So what: Points to a structural, multi-year demand shift toward European-built military hardware and capability gap-fillers (land forces, navy, cyber, space) as US-provided capabilities are withdrawn — a specific sourcing/localization theme for European defense contractors.
The US is buying 11 icebreakers under a bilateral defense/industrial arrangement with Finland, with 4 built in Finland and 7 built in the United States.
data_point🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highUnited StatesFinlandicebreakers
So what: A concrete, named cross-border defense-industrial order (Arctic/icebreaker capacity) that signals US reliance on Finnish shipbuilding expertise — a specific data point for Arctic infrastructure and shipyard order-book tracking.
Stubb predicts Ukraine achieves a 'defense victory' (preserved sovereignty and independence, not necessarily territorial reconquest), noting Ukraine is currently stronger militarily, politically and financially than at any prior point in the war, while Russia's battlefield and economic losses are outpacing Ukraine's.
prediction🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highUkraineRussia
So what: A senior European head-of-state's base case for war outcome and relative attrition, useful context for sizing Ukraine reconstruction, European sanctions-policy durability, and Russia-exposed asset risk.
≈ “I see a defense victory in the cards for Ukraine.” paraphrase
Ukraine War Endgame Mechanics 3
Over half of the Russian population now wants the war to end, and Stubb argues the war will conclude not from economic collapse or bank runs, nor from battlefield losses alone, but from domestic political pressure on the Russian regime.
prediction🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: mediumRussiaUkraine
So what: Suggests investors should watch Russian domestic sentiment/polling and internal political signals as the leading indicator for war termination, more than sanctions-driven economic indicators or front-line maps.

“Ukraine is successfully hitting oil refineries, which then hits the gas pump of the Ukrainians where they have rationing and limits.” ▶ 28:36
The line between war and peace is 'blurred' by hybrid warfare — undersea cable cutting, drone incursions over airports, GPS jamming, cyberattacks and sabotage attributed to Russia and other state/non-state actors — which Stubb says does not yet meet the threshold for NATO Article 5 activation.
data_point🗣 Alexander Stubbconviction: highRussiaNATOhybrid warfare

California Regulatory & Fiscal Burden 8
Chief Executive Magazine has ranked California dead last (50th of 50 states) for business climate in its annual survey of business leaders for more than a decade running, despite the state's size and wealth.
data_point🗣 Steve Hiltonconviction: highCaliforniaChief Executive Magazine

“Chief Executive Magazine has ranked California in its annual survey of business leaders for more than a decade 50th out of 50 for business climate.” ▶ 3:23
California currently has over 420,000 active state regulations, and gubernatorial candidate Steve Hilton has pledged to cut that to under 200,000 by the end of his first term if elected.
prediction🗣 Steve Hiltonconviction: mediumCaliforniaSteve Hilton
So what: A greater-than-50% cut to the state regulatory code, if achieved, would be a major deregulatory catalyst lowering compliance and litigation costs for California-based operators — an election-contingent event worth tracking into November.
“we're going to cut the number of regulations in California from where we are today, which is over 420,000 by the end of my first term to under 200,000, which makes us competitive with the rest of the country.” ▶ 6:23
Businesses that start or operate in California face roughly a 90% chance of ending up in litigation, which Hilton attributes to the state's uniquely aggressive plaintiff's-bar and regulatory environment.
data_point🗣 Steve Hiltonconviction: mediumCalifornia
So what: Litigation exposure should be explicitly underwritten as a cost line for any company with meaningful California operations or employment footprint.
“You run a business in California. You start a business in California, 90% chance you're going to end up in litigation.” ▶ 5:06
California's state budget has roughly doubled over the last 10 years even as an estimated two million people have left the state, meaning real per-resident government spending has surged sharply while the tax base has shrunk.
causal🗣 Steve Hiltonconviction: highCalifornia
So what: A doubled budget funded by a shrinking population implies continued upward pressure on state taxes and fees — a structural headwind for California cost-of-living and corporate tax exposure.
“They've doubled the budget of the state of California in the last 10 years with two million people having left the state. Everything's gotten worse.” ▶ 14:06
California's median home price is nearly $1 million while the median individual salary is about $65,000, an affordability gap Hilton cites as evidence the state's housing market is broken and driving out-migration.
data_point🗣 Steve Hiltonconviction: highCalifornia
So what: A roughly 15x price-to-income ratio supports continued middle-income out-migration and points to a two-tier real estate market where entry-level/middle housing demand keeps eroding even as high-end coastal property stays insulated.
“the median house price in California is nearly a million dollars. The median salary in California for an individual 65,000.” ▶ 10:06
Net private-sector job creation in California has been basically zero since the pandemic, even though the state touts having the world's fourth-largest economy — a gap Hilton attributes to GDP being inflated by the growing size of government itself.
contrarian🗣 Steve Hiltonconviction: highCalifornia
So what: Headline state GDP figures may overstate underlying private-sector health; investors should discount California 'growth' narratives that lean on aggregate GDP rather than private employment and output data.
“The figure for net job private sector job creation since the pandemic in California is basically zero. It's a disaster economically, but we're kept afloat by that one sector.” ▶ 20:12
Peter Thiel's framing, relayed by David Sacks, holds that Silicon Valley acts as a 'resource curse' for California — generating so much private wealth and tax revenue that state government has no real incentive to govern well.
framework🗣 David Sacksconviction: mediumPeter ThielSilicon ValleyCaliforniaDavid Sacks
So what: Useful mental model for why concentrated tech wealth in a jurisdiction does not automatically translate into good governance or business climate — applicable when evaluating other emerging tech-wealth hubs.
“One observation Peter Till made years ago was that Silicon Valley was like a resource curse for the state of California because it generates so much wealth and taxes that they don't need a good government here.” ▶ 18:53
A 2018 California law legalizing sidewalk street vending statewide stripped cities and counties of authority to regulate it, enabling an estimated 50,000 unpermitted vendors in LA (permit cost just $21) to undercut tax-paying brick-and-mortar retailers who bear full tax, rent, electric and licensing costs — illustrated by a 14-year-old family-run shoe store in South Central LA being driven toward closure by untaxed street competition.
causal🗣 Steve Hiltonconviction: highCaliforniaLos Angeles
So what: A concrete example of regulatory arbitrage eroding formal small-business economics in California metros — relevant to municipal tax-base and commercial-vacancy modeling.
“this 14-year business, a lovely little local business, everything spotless, everything clean... is going out of business because you got people there that are not paying tax, not paying electric, not paying rent, not complying with any licensing.” ▶ 30:26
Energy & Fuel Cost Policy 3
California retail gas prices (around $6/gallon vs. roughly $3/gallon elsewhere) are driven substantially by state policy layers on top of crude and refining costs — Hilton names the Low Carbon Fuel Standard and the Cap-and-Invest program as major hidden-tax components, with Cap-and-Invest revenue funding the high-speed rail project.
causal🗣 Steve Hiltonconviction: highCaliforniahigh-speed rail
So what: A change in state leadership repealing these programs could materially cut California retail gas prices, with direct read-through to consumer discretionary spending and to the funding continuity of the high-speed rail project.
“The low carbon fuel standard, the cap and invest program. That's about a $150 on the gas price right there. Guess where that money goes? The cap and invest program. That's what's funding highspeed rail.” ▶ 15:21
California vehicle registration fees run $800-$1,000+ for many drivers versus under $100 in most other states; Hilton's platform proposes capping registration at a flat $73 per vehicle per year by abolishing the DMV.
prediction🗣 Steve Hiltonconviction: mediumCaliforniaDMV
So what: Signals a specific, concrete potential policy shift that would remove a large recurring fee burden on California vehicle owners and fleets if enacted — an election-contingent catalyst.
“we are going to cap vehicle registration at the flat registration rate of $73 per vehicle per year by abolishing the DMV.” ▶ 15:55
Hilton's energy platform argues California imports oil from halfway around the world while restricting in-state production, a policy he claims paradoxically raises net emissions from shipping while raising consumer costs, and proposes increasing use of California's own energy resources instead.
thesis🗣 Steve Hiltonconviction: mediumCalifornia
So what: If adopted, this stance could be favorable for in-state California oil and gas producers currently constrained by permitting restrictions — worth watching alongside the election outcome.
“Instead of shipping oil halfway around the world pretending that we're doing something about climate change, even though we're actually increasing emissions by doing that, we're going to use California energy resources.” ▶ 14:43
